## BY JOHANN VALENTIN
### ANDREAE
<note>Johann Valentin Andreae (1586–1654) was a prominent German theologian, social reformer, and writer. He is most famous for his roles in the Rosicrucian movement and his utopian work, *Christianopolis*.</note>

<note>original: "Memorialia"</note>

## GIVEN TO THE

### HONOR, LOVE, AND SHARED
### GRIEF OF WELL-WISHERS.

<note>Tobias Hess (1568–1614) was Andreae’s close friend and mentor in the "Tübingen Circle." He is often linked to the early development of Rosicrucian ideas.</note>

### *To the Sincere Reader.*
<note>original: "Candido Lectori." A common Latin greeting to a fair-minded or "bright" reader.</note>

I must entreat you, kind Reader, to grant some indulgence to my affection in these *Memorials* <note>original: "Memorialibus." These are the commemorative essays and funeral orations listed in the contents.</note>. For I am well aware that I was gripped by either love or grief when I wrote these things: nor am I so made of iron that I feel no leaning toward my benefactors, or look upon public disaster <note>original: "cladem." This likely refers to the "Fire of Vaihingen" mentioned on the previous page, which devastated Andreae's parish.</note> unmoved—something, however, which a person who does not know these friends of mine, or these works of mine, might easily do. Yet I would dare to profess that no injury has been done to the truth <note>original: "veritati." Andreae is defending his objectivity; though he feels deep emotion, he claims the historical facts remain accurate.</note>, and I can promise many witnesses. For what benefit to others these works should see the light of day <note>A common idiom for being published.</note>, it is not clear; I certainly, with my well-wishers...

<summary>In this preface, Johann Valentin Andreae asks the reader for indulgence, explaining that his writings are born of deep personal grief and affection for his patrons and his community, yet he maintains they are truthful accounts.</summary>

<keywords>preface, Johann Valentin Andreae, memorial, truthfulness, emotional writing, 17th-century literature</keywords>

...I certainly, with my well-wishers, remember with joy, and I gratefully acknowledge Divine protection <note>original: "Divinam protectionem."</note> in that double danger <note>original: "geminato illo periculo." This likely refers to the traumatic fires Andreae survived, specifically the 1634 destruction of Vaihingen during the Thirty Years' War.</note>. If, however, I may also achieve this one thing—that I might remind you of death and prepare you for life—let that be accomplishment enough for these meager pages.

->Farewell, mindful of good men.<-
<note>original: "Vale, bonorum memor." A closing salutation. "Bonorum" can refer to "good things" (blessings) or "good men" (the virtuous subjects of these memorials).</note>

# TO THE MOST NOBLE YOUTH, THE LORD PHILIP
## of GEMMINGEN, in Rappenau and Buttenhausen, etc.

->Sends his heartiest greetings.<-
<note>original: "S. P. C.," an abbreviation for "Salutem Plurimam Citit" or "Salutem Precatur Conitue," a standard Latin greeting wishing much health or prosperity.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'N' containing floral and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

I do not know, most noble Philip, if I have ever experienced anything more pleasant than when we lived together in the companionship <note>original: "contubernio," a term often used for students sharing quarters or a close-knit intellectual circle</note> of that man who can never be praised enough, Lord Matthias Hafenreffer <note>Matthias Hafenreffer (1561–1619) was a prominent Lutheran theologian and a mentor to Andreae; his presence here validates the social and intellectual status of both the author and the recipient</note>, our teacher. From that time, though we were separated in body, I have kept for you an unbreakable spirit. For the piety and sweetness of your character do not allow me to forget you; and since learning and practical experience <note>original: "rerum usus," referring to the application of knowledge in worldly affairs or governance</note> have been added to those virtues, you will easily resemble your excellent father: whose image, formerly depicted by me—

<summary>This page is a formal dedication to Philipp von Gemmingen, the son of the deceased Eberhard. The author, Johann Valentin Andreae, reminisces about their shared time as students under the famous theologian Matthias Hafenreffer to establish a bond of "true nobility" through shared education and virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Philipp von Gemmingen, Matthias Hafenreffer, Rappenau, Buttenhausen, student life, dedication, virtue, education</keywords>

# PREFACE

*...I now send <note>The word fragment "sam" likely concludes "imaginem" (image/likeness) from the previous page.</note>, as a witness of my gratitude toward your father and my perpetual affection toward you. Nothing here is disguised <note>original: "fucatum," literally "painted" or "dyed," meaning something artificial or deceptive</note>; everything is candid and open, which is the hereditary virtue of your family. I pray to God that, by this example, you may proceed toward all that is best, and that you may possess and continue the praiseworthy reputation of your ancestors. Farewell, most excellent Philip, and be well. At Vaihingen <note>original: "Vaibingae." Vaihingen an der Enz, where Andreae served as a superintendent and pastor.</note>. January 1, 1619.*

->From your<-

->ever faithful,<-

->Johann Valentin Andreae.<-

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the main body of the text following the dedication to Philipp von Gemmingen. The previous page concluded Andreae’s personal address, signed from Vaihingen in 1619. This page shifts to a philosophical reflection on the fragility of the state and the rare nature of virtuous leadership.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>T R U E</header>

<image-desc>decorative woodcut banner with scrolling leaf and floral patterns</image-desc>

<image-desc>ornamental woodcut initial C with foliate background</image-desc>SINCE the benefit of Virtue <note>original: "Virtutis"</note> among mortals is so great that—even when we are unaware or unobserving—the entire weight of the Commonwealth <note>original: "Reipublicæ." This refers to the state or the public body as a whole.</note> rests upon it, and indeed often leans upon the shoulders of just one or two good men, we rightly cry out: *“Alas, how painful it is that while the Commonwealth is eternal, those who govern it best are not!”*

Such is our misfortune: not only can we not keep those immortal who are the guardians and stabilizers of the public good <note>original: "salutis publicæ"</note>, but even while we have them, we fail to recognize them. We do not notice how they sustained such a heavy burden, nor do we keep the system they established in good repair. Thus, once they are called away <note>A euphemism for death.</note>, we are like people who have lost our sight; we immediately wander astray and fall into the pits of disorder, upheavals, and reckless innovation <note>original: "innovationum." In the 17th century, "innovation" often implied a dangerous or unauthorized change to a stable system.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Hence</meta>

<summary>Andreae reflects on the essential role of virtuous leaders in sustaining the state, lamenting that their value is often only recognized after they are gone, leading to social collapse.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, commonwealth, leadership, public good, social stability, mortality, governance</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflection from page 8, moving from the general neglect of virtuous leaders to a specific tribute to Eberhard von Gemmingen. It contrasts the public's obsession with superficial displays—weddings, battles, and wealth—with the quiet, essential work of moral virtue.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>NOBILITY.</header>
<sig>A 5</sig>

Hence, among us there is frequent record-keeping of pageants <note>original: "Pomparum." This refers to public processions or displays of wealth.</note>, weddings, and solemnities; an immortal memory of buildings and battles; and a perennial fame for wit, genius, and the arts. Yet for true Virtue <note>original: "Virtutis." Capitalized in the original to denote it as a personified ideal.</note>, Simplicity, and the Right—to which, nevertheless, we owe whatever good remains anywhere on earth—there is no commendation. Not even in literature itself, to which this task is properly entrusted, is there much support. Instead, wealth, titles, and the extravagance of intellects resound from the pulpits <note>original: "suggestis." This can refer to church pulpits or any raised platform for public speaking.</note>. Those arts by which the unruly common people are restrained, and especially by which the wild and wandering human soul is tamed <note>original: "cicuratur." A term often used for taming wild animals.</note>, are rarely heard. Rather, those who have toiled in vain for some time at such a thankless task, having sought and obtained release from God, fly away from the earth and from our memory alike.

Thus among us until now, though not long enough, lived he who was once the glory of Virtue, literature, solid Nobility, and of his most illustrious family: **Eberhard von Gemmingen of Rappenau and Buttenhausen, etc.** <note>Eberhard von Gemmingen (1567–1611) was a high-ranking official and landowner in the Kraichgau region of Germany.</note> who, because [he stood] in a lofty...

<summary>Andreae laments that society honors superficial glory like battles and wealth while ignoring the quiet virtue that actually sustains the world. He introduces Eberhard von Gemmingen as a rare example of true, "solid" nobility who has recently passed away.</summary>
<keywords>social critique, vanity, virtue, true nobility, Eberhard von Gemmingen, Rappenau, Buttenhausen, record-keeping</keywords>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>->T R U E<-</header>

and of excellent character <note>original: "ingenio." This refers to both natural talent and moral disposition.</note>, with an infinite knowledge <note>original: "scientia."</note> and experience of things, the applause of Fortune, an abundance of possessions <note>original: "abundantia."</note>, and the richest material for glory, fame, and memory everywhere. He sought nothing for himself except the title of the best citizen toward his country <note>original: "patria."</note>, the best Father toward his family and subjects, and the best patron toward all good men—and he obtained these most abundantly. Yet now, after he has been received by God, he seems to have departed in such a way that he has not obtained a sufficiently ample gratitude or memory worthy of his merits from literature (to which he devoted his whole life), nor from the learned (to whom he gave all his affection), nor from Virtue <note>original: "Virtute."</note> (to which he gave all his practice), nor from good men (to whom he gave all his labors), nor even from Religion <note>original: "Religione."</note> (to which he gave all his zeal). Instead, he seems to remain hidden only in the hearts and sighs of those who mourn and long for him.

And yet, in so small a number of good men in our age, if any such person should happen upon our most wretched lands, everyone ought to acclaim him; if anyone should be snatched away, everyone should wail. With such great sagacity, the footsteps of those who walked rightly [should be] tracked <note>The text cuts off here at "in-", likely "indaganda" or "investiganda," meaning "to be tracked" or "investigated."</note>...

<meta>catchword: tracked-</meta>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 10, completing the argument that the lives of virtuous men should be studied to avoid the "monstrous" paths of vanity. It specifically holds up Eberhard von Gemmingen as the ideal model of this quiet, practical nobility.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>NOBILITY.</header>
<sig>A 6</sig>

...to be tracked down <note>Continuing from the previous page: "indaganda," meaning to be investigated or tracked.</note>, so that the same path by which they were so greatly and so often beneficial might be continued, and that we might not slip away into the pompous, extravagant, monstrous, vain, and tyrannical paths of others. Instead, with a fresh memory always kept of the comfort and happiness that flows from what is simple, right, and good, we should be spurred on to preserve their precepts, as well as to record and renew them as often as possible—both for the glory of those who came before us and for the benefit of those who follow. 

Would that our Eberhard, that once extraordinary traveler on the "Right Way" <note>original: "Rectæ viæ"</note>, might find such a chronicler or eulogist, and that his holy and useful life—which was practical rather than merely showy—might be shown to posterity by some eloquent tongue. 

Truly, we would then possess—if not a model of courtly splendor, military strength, literary fame, or an extravagant life—at least the near-perfect example of the best Man, the best husband, the best father, the best master, the best Magistrate <term>Magistratus: a high-ranking civil officer or local administrator</term>, and the best Patron. In this one man, we would behold the image of a manifold ***Nobility*** <note>original: "Nobilitatis"</note> and a solid, multifaceted excellence.

<summary>This page concludes the argument for preserving the legacies of virtuous men, presenting Eberhard von Gemmingen as a "near-perfect" example of domestic and civic virtue over superficial courtly display.</summary>
<keywords>Eberhard von Gemmingen, virtue, nobility, magistrate, domestic life, role models, Recta Via, civic duty</keywords>

<page-num>12</page-num>

-># TRUE<-

<meta>The header "TRUE" (VERA) combined with the previous page's header "NOBILITY" completes the title of the work or section: "True Nobility."</meta>

And indeed, we shall look for, hope for, desire, and demand such a mirror of a fruitful life <note>original: "vitæ fructiferæ"</note> from someone among that number of most learned scholars <note>original: "eruditissimorum"</note> whom he favored. But as for me—since, by God's favor, I was permitted to enjoy the acquaintance, favor, and familiarity of such a great man (though, alas, for only a short time)—even though I am destitute of all that eloquence required to praise the praiseworthy, I am nonetheless so full of tears, devoted affection, the memory of favors received, the recollection of the most pleasant conversation, and admiration for such great Virtue—as well as a readiness to serve the children he left behind—that I could not restrain myself from pouring all this out. 

I offer this to his son Philip <note>Philip von Gemmingen (1569–1595), who continued the family line</note>, that most fortunate mirror of his father’s countenance and character, and to all you good people who shall read this. I am ready to endure any reputation rather than fail to devote everything my stammering and sterile talent can achieve toward restoring and bringing forward the course of this praiseworthy life <term>vitæ curriculum: the "racecourse" or career of a life; the origin of the modern "CV"</term>, thereby rescuing from oblivion this most exquisite example of Nobility—

<page-num>13</page-num>

# NOBILITY.

—a specimen of Nobility <note>original: "Nobilitatis specimen"; this completes the sentence from the previous page</note>, a paternal and domestic example of virtue to be displayed to his son; and to commend to everyone a true life and the duty of a man on earth. That I may be able to achieve this, I pray from my heart for God’s favor.

In the Noble Life of Eberhard von Gemmingen, therefore, I do not know whether I should first admire his **Fortune** or the merit of that **Fortune** <term>Fortuna: in this context, it refers to both worldly success and the favor of Divine Providence</term>; they competed so much with one another: Fortune by bestowing every desired and excellent thing, and he himself by seeking and earning them through praiseworthy effort. Yet, the former was certainly prior—that is, the manifest and almost unique favor of God, with which He has most illustriously embraced the Gemmingen family for several centuries, and which He has most kindly continued through so many grandfathers and parents even to our own age. Within that family history, there are many examples of ancient bravery, genuine integrity, Noble Constancy, most abundant wealth, the highest honors, sublime and electoral dignities <note>original: "electoralium"; the Gemmingen family frequently served as high-ranking advisors and officials to the Prince-Electors of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, and most splendid structures—

<meta>catchword: na</meta>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Gemmingen family's legacy from page 13, finishing the word "splendidissima" (most splendid structures). It transitions from the general virtues of the lineage to the specific birth and early character of Eberhard von Gemmingen.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

<header>T R U E</header>

—structures and examples; but I know not by what secret of the **House of Gemmingen**, one may see within it more instances of generosity, devotion, thrift, integrity, kindness, favor toward the good, love toward subjects, approachability toward individuals, and a dovelike *uprightness and simplicity* <note>original: "simplicitatisque columbinæ"; an allusion to Matthew 10:16, "be wise as serpents and innocent as doves"</note> which have so propagated themselves even to this day into branches and shoots, that if it pleases others elsewhere to boast of different ancestral images, these men can confidently, and would gladly, boast of these characters of their ancestors.

Born of this most fortunate stock, but descended from parents far more fortunate, he first saw the *light of day* <note>original: "Lucem primam aspicit"</note> at *Bürg* <note>original: "Burgii"; refers to Schloss Bürg, a family castle near Neuenstadt am Kocher</note> on [July 16, 1567], in the bright *Light of the Gospel* <note>A reference to the Protestant Reformation, which was firmly established in the region</note> and in that famous hostel of light, *Württemberg*, the seat of his *grandfather Eberhard*, or rather, the refuge of all good men in exile. He was the heir to both the arts of war and to bravery, and to the opportunities for ascending to all the most famous heights; as soon as he grasped the paths of domestic thrift and the duty of a good head of the household, he thereafter held onto them so tenaciously <note>original: "mordicus"; literally "with his teeth"</note> that, just as they are the only [means] of a quiet, comfortable, and unimpeded life,

<meta>This page continues the biography of Eberhard von Gemmingen (1567–1611), picking up from the previous page's discussion of his dedication to domestic thrift and virtue as the foundation of a stable life.</meta>

# NOBILITY.

found the means of a blessed life; so also thereafter he exchanged those values for no treasures or splendor. He found them to be perpetually fertile sources of gold, honor, peace, reputation, and finally, eternal salvation.

That domestic realm, as it were, and the excellent administration of a small Republic <note>In the 16th and 17th centuries, a nobleman’s estate was often described as a "small republic," serving as a model for how a kingdom should be governed through order and morality.</note> brought forth peace, joy, and that famous Harmony <note>original: "Concordiam"; a central Renaissance virtue involving social and familial unity.</note> which is still spoken of today. Not only did the subjects find rest and growth, rejoicing and congratulating themselves on the protection, help, counsel, instruction, and example of their Magistrate; not only did the male and female servants and attendants perform their duties and establish a peaceful and religious household; but the heads of the family themselves—the father, I say, and the three sons mature in years (indeed, fathers of numerous offspring themselves)—agreed so well among themselves and conspired together *in the peace of virtue, and in that uprightness and simplicity characteristic of the House of Gemmingen,* <note>original: "Gemmingensium proprio, simplici & recto"; this refers back to the "dovelike simplicity" mentioned on page 14 as a family trait.</note> to choose wives for themselves according to that virtue, and in it to nourish, educate, and entirely accustom their "little flock" <note>original: "pusillum gregem"; a common biblical reference to one's children or congregation, from Luke 12:32.</note>. No dissolute life was seen there, no blasphemous tongue, no distended or luxurious belly; no insane [agitation] <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "gita-" suggests the word "exagitatio" (agitation or excitement).</note> of horses—

<meta>catchword: gita-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the orderly and harmonious administration of the Gemmingen household, portraying it as a "small republic" where the father and his three sons lived in virtuous unity. It emphasizes the moral environment of the estate, contrasting it with the typical excesses of noble life like gluttony or blasphemy.</summary>

Indeed, there was great virtue in the Father and in all the Sons; but just as among many most elegant forms, each possesses its own peculiar and excellent beauty—a "victory" for each individual—so the one we now observe more closely, *Reinhard*, the middle brother, is uniquely pleasing and seems to deserve more extensive praise. Since his father’s blessed departure, he has been most wealthy in the inheritance <term>hæreditate (inheritance)</term> of all the best virtues—especially those characteristic of the **Gemmingen family**—and of their character. ## NOBILITY.

...anxious lest he fail to be as useful as possible to the Commonwealth <note>original: "Reipublicæ"; referring to the state or public welfare.</note>, since examples of both kinds of virtue—that of the gown <term>togatæ (civilian or scholarly service)</term> and that of arms <term>armatæ (military service)</term>—were abundant. Consequently, he inclined toward letters, so that he might behold in his sons that which he had hitherto admired and fostered in others—but which he himself, to his sorrow, lacked—and might rejoice in so great an addition to the ancestral virtues within his own house, and in such a magnificent ornament.

The choice followed: and his prayers were heard by God even more fully than he had dared to hope. That [virtue], which they sucked in with their first milk, which they heard with their first words, for which they were shaped in their first and tender infancy, to which they were later won over by faithful and fatherly explanation, and which they finally converted into their very blood and marrow—

### NOBILITY.

Our eldest, ***Eberhard***, does not merely exceed the hopes of his friends and the desires of his teachers; he surpasses even himself. That is to say, although his memory initially seemed ill-equipped to match his great hunger for knowledge and his efforts in learning, he applied himself so fervently that, through sheer diligence, he was rendered equal and closest to the progress of even the most gifted students. In this way, the foundations—or rather the first principles—of his "literary edifice" <note>original: "literarij ædificij"; an architectural metaphor for a complete and structured education.</note> were laid. His memory was exercised, his judgment sharpened, and above all, he practiced the "preludes" of becoming a ***Good Man***—one destined to one day walk the straight path of virtue.

These studies took place at ***Neuenstadt on the Kocher*** <note>original: "Neostadio ad Cocheram"; a town in Baden-Württemberg where the family held influence.</note> until, having mastered <note>original: "devoratis"; literally "having devoured," implying a rapid and thorough consumption of knowledge.</note> the difficult basics of languages and the more taxing elements of the liberal arts, he was deemed worthy to enter the more pleasant life of the ***University*** <term>Academicam (referring to higher university education, distinct from preparatory schooling)</term>. ***Tübingen*** was the first of all to receive him—a city rightly celebrated for its sincere ***Religion***, its wealth of highly learned men, and its dedication to the highest pursuits. However, he was not sent there to live as one who might waste money in a profligate manner, or who would acknowledge no laws—

A father orders one set of things to be purchased, a son desires another, but the **University** offers something else entirely: namely, the conversation of the most learned men, a selection of the most exquisite books, an abundance of the most ingenious inventions, the opportunity for the most useful exercises, the comfort of the most pleasant friendships, and the testing and experience of various minds. It offers countless other most elegant things which could shape a life with decency, adorn it with elegance, and delight it appropriately—liberally repaying any expenses incurred.

Truly happy is **Eberhard**, for whom it was not permitted to purchase foreign vices <note>original: *exotica mala*; literally "exotic evils"</note> at a high price—nor perhaps was it even his pleasure to do so. ...who at last departed from his teachers and all his friends in such a way that neither did they regret the services they had rendered, nor was he himself ever forgetful of the favors he had received.

Was he then finished with his studies? Not at all, for he had taken them on as lifelong companions, as mistresses of his counsel, and as guides for his reason; yet of that time which his Father had decreed to be given to academic life, it was enough. Now the father's elderly shoulders seemed to be growing weary, and the son’s manly shoulders seemed equal to the burden; his talents were now mature enough to overflow for the benefit of his subjects.

A wife was chosen for him. It was not her lineage, nor her beauty, nor her wealth, nor the elegance of her manners—however much she possessed these in abundance—that had recommended her to his Father, but rather (to use the deceased's own words) her mature age, her simple clothing, her readiness for labor, her slightly hardened hands <note>This description emphasizes the Protestant or traditional value of a hardworking wife over one of mere vanity or leisure.</note>, and the sincerity of her words. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And so, at this point, we shall begin the account of Eberhard’s life, which we measure only by his exercise of virtue and his service to his people. Furthermore, as for his progress in his scholarly <note>original: *literaria*; literally "literary," but here referring to his university studies and academic career.</note> life—which his superiors promoted, his inferiors admired, his peers loved, and all honored together—he himself regarded these as preparations for a true life. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In this way, divine <term>blessing</term> <note>original: *benedictio*</note> was so administered that it was never closed off to any good person, yet was not open to the wicked. It was a place where modest and sober friends might turn, where the afflicted and the miserable might find refuge, and where the <term>learned</term> <note>original: *literati*; referring specifically to those educated in the humanities and classical letters</note> and the gifted might approach,

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Meanwhile, the fertile Mother <note>Anna Catharina von Rotenstein</note> crowned the Father <note>Eberhard</note> eight times with noble offspring; yet, as if she had accomplished too little by birth alone, she adorned these gifts of hers with such great care and concern that even while they were still mute, tiny, and babbling, she had already shaped and trained them toward the praise of God, reverence for their parents, and the foundations of civilized behavior, bringing them up with the Father's deepest affection.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then, as if a division of labor had been made, she took the daughters to herself to be trained in household management, modest conduct, and every virtuous refinement; she handed the sons over to the Father, so that he might educate them not only in the first elements of literature, but also—as he diligently provided for his son Philip—in the foundations of the arts. Indeed, so great was the passion for imitation on both sides <note>among both the sons and the daughters</note> that they feared they might lack some virtue for which their parents were famous, or be found inferior in the competition for integrity and good reputation.

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Eberhard von Gemmingen’s domestic governance. Having established in the previous page that he built his castle and filled it with virtues, the text now addresses why he chose the quiet management of his own estates over the "noise" and distractions of the high princely courts.</meta>

# NOBILITY.

...the noise of the Court? Let that be for others, and let it please others; but truly, for a man to whom so many people—as were once committed by God to our **Eberhard**—have been entrusted, and who has resolved to embrace them with such care and rule them with such solicitude as he did, there is no need to involve himself in the affairs of others. Nor is there any danger that he should serve God or his Fatherland with any less labor.

But let the learned dispute such matters. This man, like a father to every one of his subjects, looked first to the purity of the **Divine Word** <note>original: *Divini Verbi*; a reference to the Lutheran emphasis on "Scripture alone" and the correct teaching of the Bible in his territories.</note>, and then to peace, governance, and growth. From there, as much as he was able, he appointed proven men of upright life to lead them everywhere, or removed those who were unworthy. Soon, he inquired carefully into what the morals, labors, and <term>household management</term> <note>original: *œconomia*; in this period, the term referred to the "law of the house" or the effective running of a family estate or farm.</note> of each person were; striving with effort, he helped the good and thwarted the plans of the wicked.

His treasures were the successes of his subjects. His subjects were almost his only debtors—those who, with gratitude, credited to him their small fields and houses, the manual trades of their children, their own education, honorable marriages, their means of seeking a livelihood, and the way of living comfortably in all things. He [knew] the names of each individual...

Nor was it any less the case that if someone was called away by God <note>original: *à Deo evocandus*; a common 16th-century euphemism for death.</note>, they sought him as the best guardian for their children; if someone was embroiled in a legal dispute, they sought him as the best arbitrator; if in a conflict of minds, the best peacemaker; if the Homeland was in danger, the best counselor; in uncertain matters, the best friend; on the path of virtue, the best guide; and finally, if anyone desired the best **Patron** to support literature and the <term>humanities</term> <note>original: *humanioribus*; referring to the "studia humanitatis," the classical arts and literature that formed the core of Renaissance education.</note>, they came here. Here, their needs were met; indeed, the hopes of every individual were surpassed. For he was a man who had dedicated his entire life to doing good, choosing—above all glory, or the fame of a name, and through every labor, every hardship, and even the hatred of certain people—to help, benefit, and advise those who made worthy requests.

judgment with <term>piety</term> <note>original: *pietas*; a core Renaissance virtue combining religious devotion with a sense of duty to family and community.</note>, and had bestowed whatever ornaments or excellence there were from every side upon that which was simple and upright. Neither his noble lineage, nor a more liberated way of life, nor wealth, nor the applause of Fortune or fame could cause harm here; instead, <term>reason</term> <note>original: *ratio*; referring here to the Stoic or Christian-humanist ideal of a life guided by rational principle rather than impulse.</note>, which he had absorbed throughout his whole life, predominated. From this reason came a daily sustenance that cost none of his subjects their hunger; clothing that cost no one their nakedness; activities that cost no one their exhaustion; services that cost no one their compulsion; <term>generosity</term> <note>original: *liberalitas*; the traditional virtue of a nobleman in giving freely and hosting others.</note> that cost no one their plundering <note>original: *depilatione*; literally "plucking of hair," a metaphorical term for being fleeced or stripped of assets through taxes.</note>; an abundance that was a burden to no one; and a preeminence that consisted of no one’s oppression. The splendor and happiness of his life were joined with the well-being of every individual, never boastful or excessive. Instead, whenever he had leisure, he withdrew into books, either seeking justice and equity, or thoroughly learning about human actions and the revolutions of the centuries, or examining the discourses of <term>Political writers</term> <note>original: *Politicorum*; referring to authors of political theory, such as Aristotle, Cicero, or contemporary Renaissance theorists.</note>; but above all else, his life was devoted to the **meditation on the excellence of our Soul and the happiness obtained through Christ.**

Indeed, for her husband, subjects, relatives, and friends, there were scarcely as many days as they wished; and, if we believe the mourners, altogether fewer than was fair. He had already built a new church <note>Likely referring to the church in Bürg or a similar family estate that Eberhard renovated or built at his own expense.</note> at his own expense and completed it; he had already sent the first of his sons to his studies; he had already prepared himself in spirit and through vows for every praiseworthy endeavor, when—contrary to the hopes and prayers of all good people—his wife, **Anna Catharina**, was snatched away. She was the other half <note>original: *dimidium*; a classical allusion to Horace, who called a dear friend "the half of my soul."</note> of his heart, of his virtues, of his will, and of his most dedicated duty. 

She had been troubled for some time by a difficult illness, yet one that seemed it might yield to remedies. After she had unsuccessfully tried the **hot springs of Baden** <note>original: *Badenfes thermas*; referring to the famous spa town of Baden-Baden, known for its medicinal thermal waters.</note>, and as the most acute headaches took hold, she was confined to bed for a few days. She most peacefully took her last breath amidst the prayers and tears of her family. [October 3, 1611, at 7:00 PM].

The mourning here was immense; it seemed greatest to all those who, through the kindness of such a noble Lady, had either survived at all or had lived more comfortably. The grief was greatest for the family, which until now had leaned upon her as a pillar; it was greatest for the children, for whom from this point on...

This was the cause of our own sorrow—an excessive grief that brought forth eternal mourning for us. He, who until now had harmed no one, wounded us with incurable wounds through his final tears, groans, and sighs.

...having left behind all his friends and nearly spurning us, he hurries toward his wife, toward the wife so often called upon, toward his most longed-for wife.

In this one thing alone he loved not for himself, but too much for his own people; he bound himself by the bond of marriage and harmony more tightly than nature had required. But for him—with whom and for whose sake he would have lived gladly, and without whom living was a burden and something he did not wish—it was as if he were eager to bear witness to something he owed to the departed spirit <note>original: "Manibus"; in Roman tradition, the Manes were the spirits of the dead. He did not shudder at death; he did not feel the pains of the disease; he was not touched by concern for his children; he was not moved by the tears of his kin. Christ, to him (whose most holy body [he received] most devotedly...

...had received me) he had already made death a thing to be longed for. The expectation of what was to come had eased his illness. Confession, repentance, and faith had laid the burden <note>original: "sarcinam"; literally a soldier's pack or bundle, suggesting the heavy weight of sin that a traveler must set down.</note> of his sins at the feet of the Redeemer. His reputation was secured by the conscience of a life lived rightly and the testimony of good men. His children were sufficiently provided for under the protection of God. At that moment, like one ready for a journey and prepared to enter the heavenly life, he desired to say his final farewell to his family.

His most sorrowful brother, Johannes Wilhelmus, was present, along with three most mournful sisters who had gathered for this unexpected and tragic farewell. The dying man, the most steadfast of all and of a most composed mind, did not need comfort; instead, he offered it. He did not anxiously beg for the physician’s hand <note>original: "Medicam manum"; a reference to medical intervention. In the "art of dying" (ars moriendi) tradition, the dying person often turns away from earthly medicine to focus on spiritual healing.</note> but barely tolerated it. He did not seek even a few more days of life, but instead pointed out the hour of his departure. 

As all who were able to witness this with their own eyes testify, he acted no differently than a man in perfect health who wished to resign his office into the hands of others and depart. Having prayed that God would be a favorable guardian to his children, he left behind an honorable memory of himself, and for his children...

# NOBILITAS.
<note>This header completes the phrase "VERA NOBILITAS" (True Nobility) from the previous page's header.</note>

...was bound by that debt [of nature], he also earned praise for his readiness and courage. Shall we then grieve that he has died as a man, when as a heavenly soul he has been received into the heavens? As a perfect lover, he has been drawn to his Beloved <note>Likely referring to the soul's union with God or Christ</note>; as a servant of all things good, he has been manumitted <term>manumission: the formal act of freeing a slave</term>; as a prisoner of his own body, he has been set free. Finally, shall we grieve that a good man, citizen, husband, father, magistrate, patron, friend, and master has been called away to receive the rewards of his virtues: the crown of Faith and piety, and the prize <note>original: "brabeum," a Greek-derived term for a victor’s trophy or prize</note> of patience and constancy?

But indeed we mourn, and we—so many of us—deplore our lot, because people from almost every walk of life feel all the more deeply what we have lost. The house is not merely empty of a spouse or of the man himself; rather, with his departure, that lately conspicuous chorus of virtues has also left us. The family mourns an honorable branch; the house mourns its strongest pillar; the orphans mourn their protector <note>original: "parentes," used here in the sense of a parental guardian</note>; the subjects mourn a father; the servants mourn a father; the afflicted mourn their refuge; the Universities mourn an illustrious and grateful student; the State <term>Respublica: the "common-weal" or public state</term> mourns its guardian.

O happy souls, happy bodies, now joined once again, rest! You have labored enough, now rest; you have done good in abundance, now rest; and enjoy your most blessed marriage in the heavens. Now nothing shall separate you, but in eternal union in Christ, you shall await that part of you which remains on earth <note>Referring to the physical bodies left in the tomb or perhaps their living descendants</note>. The cycle of your lives is complete; you have now finished your service <term>ministerio: refers here to his official duties and his "service" to God on earth</term>. No longer will heavy cares or painful diseases trouble you, but in blessed rest and restful blessedness, while the rest of the world continues to turn, you shall sleep. And at the glorious sound of the trumpet, called back by God, you shall receive your souls as they awaken; having received them, you shall be led before God; having been led forth, you shall be justified <note>original: "excusabimini," meaning to be vindicated or cleared of charges at the Final Judgment</note> by Christ; and having been justified, you shall be admitted to all heavenly joys,

...in the company of the saints, the choirs of Angels, the perpetual sight of the Trinity, and with eternal applause <note>original: "applausibus," here meaning joyful acclamation or celebration</note>, you shall enjoy yourselves forever.
O blessed death <term>mors beata: a "happy death," occurring in a state of grace and ending worldly suffering</term>, the end of our miseries, the doorway to true life, the only medicine for the most suffering body! No one hates you, no one fears you, and no one judges you deformed <note>The author argues that death is only "ugly" or frightening to those who lack virtue</note>—provided they make good reason, a pure conscience <term>conscientia: the inner moral sense of right and wrong</term>, and true liberty the rules of their life. Such a person never submits to the opinions, the storms, or the customs of men, but instead examines and guides all their thoughts, desires, impulses, and actions toward what is *simple and right*. They judge no art to be more exquisite, no knowledge more excellent, no fame more desirable, no title more sublime, and no Fortune <term>Fortuna: the personified force of luck or fate, often depicted as fickle</term> more generous than **TO BE GOOD**—and thus they experience it throughout the entire course of their life. From this, such a person recognizes that nothing under the heavens is truly their own, but that all things are foreign <note>original: "aliena," meaning belonging to another, or transitory</note> and merely lent for use. They place no trust in the flatteries of Fortune or the empty praise <term>adulationibus: fawning or excessive flattery</term> of men, but instead recognize and find things as they truly are within themselves,

...thus he uses them outwardly, judges them, and by no means allows himself to be ruled by them; and finally, having settled the accounts of his life <note>original: "expeditâ vitæ subductione," a metaphor for balancing a ledger or completing a final reckoning</note>, he approaches death, undergoes it, and through it penetrates into life.

Such was the life of our **Eberhard**, now gloriously resting in Christ, which it is unworthy for us to dishonor with our tears and softness. He, who had fashioned his body for endurance, his mind for firmness, and his whole way of life for moderation <term>mediocritatem: the "Golden Mean," a classical ideal of avoiding extremes in behavior and emotion</term>, toward the *simple and right*, now even in death commands us to gird ourselves for a masculine imitation of his virtues rather than for womanly weeping <note>The author uses gendered rhetorical tropes common in the 16th/17th centuries, associating emotional restraint with "masculine" virtue and excessive grief with "feminine" weakness</note>.

...most desirably, we should wish with all our longing that these same virtues continue, increase, and be propagated in the noble offspring he has left behind; let us strive for this with every labor and zeal, and by this final service <term>merito: a service or duty performed to honor someone</term> to **Eberhard**, let us offer a sacrifice to his sacred and immaculate ashes.

May shade never be lacking there for the good, nor life-giving sap for the needy, nor fruit for the Church; let the shaken house be propped up by columns of blessing and divine protection, and may the virtues of **Eberhard** return to these desolate halls. 

May his guardians, his brothers, his elders, and his betters all bring a fatherly spirit, and may they bring back to us that one man who was once a father; may friends remember a friend, subjects a gracious leader <note>original: "obsequentem," suggesting a ruler who was attentive to the needs and laws of his people</note>, scholars a man of genius, and the needy a grateful heart. ...merits, let them build a **Cenotaph** <term>Cenotaphium: a monument erected in honor of a person whose body is elsewhere; here used metaphorically to mean a lasting memorial in the soul</term> within the hearts of orphans. Let all, let everyone pray for rest for the departed, and for those who remain, let them pray for a devotion to what is simple and right.

And so, farewell from me, **Most Noble Man**; farewell, my joy; farewell, my love; farewell, my hope. Because I found in you such excellence—long desired and sought after—not a painted or fictitious image of virtue, but a living example, please accept this small and truly scholarly <term>scholasticum: a formal academic tribute or oration</term> offering. Farewell in God, whom you worshipped; farewell in Christ, whom you loved; farewell in the peace you promoted; farewell in the quietude you practiced; farewell in the glory you awaited; farewell in the harmony you embodied; farewell in the knowledge you craved. Farewell, farewell, until we meet again, **Most Noble**, most prudent, most generous, most upright, most blessed

We have mourned the mediator <note>original: *pararium*; referring to a person who acts as a broker or intermediary, here meaning Tobias Hess who connected these friends</note> who was taken from us. # THE IMMORTALITY

->C. S. <note>original: *C. S.*, likely abbreviating *Cum Salute* (With Greetings) or *Christo Sacrum* (Sacred to Christ), a common devotional opening.</note><-

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter C, decorated with foliate patterns and a central flower within the curve of the letter.</image-desc>

WHEN *Tobias Hess*, who had been until now the love of the good and the object of hatred for the wicked, was recently called to God to receive the crown of Faith and Patience <note>Hess died in 1614; Andreae presents his death as a reward for his Christian endurance through various controversies.</note>, I believe there were various stirrings in the minds of many, depending on how the fame of such a great man either delighted them, or provoked them, or perhaps even gripped them under some common misunderstanding. Yet for no one else, so far as I know, did two such different feelings—an unusual love and duty toward him, combined with an intimate knowledge—seize hold at the same time as they did me: the greatest joy and the greatest grief. These feelings struck with such violence on both sides that, however monstrous it may seem to rejoice at the funeral of a very dear friend, it is truly useless—and not sufficiently pious—to mourn the blessed lot of a man who is himself now blessed. I was thus held helplessly by a sad joy and a joyful...

# OF T. HESS <note>Tobias Hess (1568–1614) was a prominent physician and scholar in Tübingen, known for his interests in alchemy and mysticism, and a mentor to the circle that produced the Rosicrucian manifestos.</note>

...[that] I may be ruled by my sadness, lest I rest in any balance of patience, but by this unusual form <note>Likely referring to the structure of the funeral oration or the book itself.</note> be driven toward the funeral rites <note>original: *iusta*</note> of a man who was once most dear to me.

Truly, this necessity of my soul, this most vehement force of love, and the clear evidence of my debt will, I believe, carry such weight with you, **Christian Reader**, that you will more gently interpret this premature fruit <note>original: *immaturum... foetum*</note> of a friend's duty. By this, I satisfy the ashes of a brother, the love of a friend, and—if that is not enough—the merits of **Hess**, not as much as I owe, but as much as I am able. I do this both unwillingly and willingly, compelled by the shadow <note>Referring to the spirit or memory of the deceased.</note> of my friend; I am deaf even to my own excuses and cannot endure myself, unless for the sake of **Hess**—alas, once mine, but now truly so—I submit to your judgment, O **Reader**, whatever it may be.

I wish that you might hold that judgment in suspense until you kindly receive, not the praises of this incomparable man, but—let it suffice to say—his character <term>ingenium: innate talent or natural character</term> and conduct <term>mores: habits, morals, or way of life</term>, however much they may be obscured by a rustic style! # IMMORTALITY

But You, **Jesus Christ**, who were until now the expectation of your **Hess** and are now his full possession; once his exercise, and now his longed-for rest; once his goal, and now his blessed union—I most ardently pray to You. You, who once tested your faithful servant for a time with almost all the instruments of Your cross, grant now at last that the example of this most invincible Athlete <note>original: *Athleta*. A common metaphor in Christian writing for a believer who undergoes intense spiritual struggle or endures persecution with the endurance of a competitor.</note> might stand—not to the infamy of those who scourged him, but as a consolation to those others under the perpetual trophy of Patience.

# T. HESS

...to persist <note>The Latin word *extare* (to persist or stand out) completes the prayer from the previous page, asking that Hess’s example remain for others.</note>, for those who, having entered the same racecourse of **sacred silence** <note>original: *sacri silentij*. This refers to the Pythagorean or mystical practice of silence, likely alluding to the secret intellectual and spiritual circles Hess moved in, such as the early Rosicrucians.</note>, strive toward that same finish line which he most successfully reached. These are the ones who, in these times, lament the ominous loss of good men. As far as the measure <note>The OCR reads *meilutum*, likely a transcription error for *modulum* (measure or small amount).</note> of my own talent allows, I have sought to trace the footsteps of this excellent man, so that those who easily dismiss inestimable men on account of unexamined trifles might, in this naked simplicity of truth, find ample reason to blush—at least as much as the reputation of others allows.

When I mention **Hess**, I know I weigh heavily upon those who knew him through love. They never remember him without pain: his countenance composed to a **Christian gravity**, his tongue most free in the **praises of God**, his heart pure <note>original: *nivei*, literally "snowy," a common metaphor for sincerity and a clear conscience.</note> toward all, and his mind most ready for anything with humility. Those who were not present to look upon the dwelling-place <note>A common philosophical reference to the physical body as the "house" of the soul.</note> of such a noble soul should imagine a man upright in body, serene of brow, lively of eye, sharp of nose, lovely of mouth, joyful of hands, strong of limb, and agile in motion. Certainly, the **riches of Nature** do not always dwell—

<meta>This page continues the description of Tobias Hess’s early life, moving from the virtuous environment of his parental home in Nuremberg to his subsequent education at several prominent German universities, which he later recalled with deep regret.</meta>

### TOBIAS HESS

...to praise [God] with hymns, to be ignorant of the malice of the world, and to abhor intemperance. Oh parents, parents, how I wish you were all worthy of such praise as our friend frequently bestowed upon his own, as he looked back on the kindness <unclear>he received</unclear>! He called them the guardians of a moderate severity and the forerunners <term>anteambulones</term> <note>Latin: *anteambulones*. In Roman life, these were servants who cleared a path through a crowd for their masters; Hess uses the term to describe his parents as those who cleared a path for him in sincere piety.</note> of sincere piety.

After this "school of the father," the Universities followed—the "profane" following the "sacred," as he often used to say. He did not attribute this disgrace to the profession of Literature and the Arts themselves, but rather to the ill-disciplined crowd that, under the pretense of scholarship, pursues license and idle luxury. These universities were Erfurt, Jena, Altorf, and Tübingen. He could never name them without a sense of horror and Christian indignation whenever he remembered, not what he *could* have achieved, but what he actually *did*. 

"How innocent I was when I left home," he would lament, "how sober, modest, and eager to learn! Yet how wicked, bold, and prone to drinking I became! I had scoundrels for companions and worthless private tutors who approved of a chaotic life; and, alas, almost no one to rebuke or advise me!"

<meta>catchword: Sacred</meta>

<summary>This page contrasts Tobias Hess's virtuous upbringing in Nuremberg with his self-described "wicked" and "drunken" years at various German universities, expressing his deep regret over the lack of moral guidance during his higher education.</summary>
<keywords>Tobias Hess, Nuremberg, Erfurt, Jena, Altorf, Tübingen, university life, piety, moral education, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the personal reflections of Tobias Hess regarding his university years. Following the previous page's mention of his "wicked and bold" behavior at Erfurt, Jena, Altorf, and Tübingen, he now details the loss of his moral compass and the influence of his peers.</meta>

-># IMMORTALITY.<-
->C 3<-

The sacred and finest books were snatched away from me and replaced by pestilential ones. In those rare moments when I regained my senses, I felt a deep ache in both body and soul. The lingering sparks of my father’s instruction tried to resist, but I was carried off by a pack of seducers—or rather, executioners <note>Latin: *lictorum*. Lictors were Roman officers who carried out sentences; Hess uses the term to suggest these "friends" were actually his punishers or captors.</note>. Though I loathed wine, I endured the pressure of those who forced it upon me. I lost the ability to blush <note>In this context, losing one's "blush" refers to the loss of natural modesty or the sense of shame that accompanies wrongdoing.</note> only against my will. In truth, I hardly heard a word of reason; instead, I grew deaf amidst the clamor of conflicting opinions. O gold, how you betrayed me! O parents, how in vain were your warnings! O virtuous people, how far you were from me!

Thus he spoke, in his extreme conscientiousness; one might have believed he had been a gambler, a glutton, a brawler, or a profligate <term>pamphilus</term> <note>Latin: *pamphilus*. A character name from Roman comedy (specifically Terence) used to denote a young man-about-town or a lover; here it implies a life of idle pleasure.</note>. Indeed, he was a judge of his youthful errors who was as much more severe toward himself as others are indulgent protectors of their own crimes. We, however, know—and men of good standing testify—that he never neglected the duties of an obedient and diligent student. Yet, because he was quite devoted to Music, he had more companions who led a loose life than a disciplined one. For this reason, he did not entirely approve of those circles—nor even of the public schools—viewing them as excellent things in principle, but [corrupted] in...

The narrator provides a counter-perspective, noting that despite Hess’s self-criticism, he remained a diligent student whose main "vice" was simply associating with less-disciplined musicians.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of Tobias Hess's intellectual pursuits. While the previous page touched on his self-criticism regarding his behavior, this page defends his academic diligence and describes his deep dissatisfaction with worldly knowledge despite his mastery of it.</meta>

# TOBIAS HESS
<meta>catchword: with them,</meta>

...turned in large part toward a terrible destruction of talents.

But who would believe that Hess was idle or dissolute? In just a few years, in addition to the most elegant triad of the Hebrew, Greek, and Latin languages, he did not merely skim through all of **Philosophy**—as our contemporaries tend to do—but drank it in deeply. Furthermore, he adorned himself so splendidly with the beauties of **Poetry** and **History**, and finally with the varied service of **Mechanics** <note>In the early modern period, "Mechanics" referred to the practical application of mathematical and physical principles, including engineering and the use of tools.</note>, that he saw few equals and infinitely many inferiors. Whether he desired **Mathematics**, or **Physics**, or the teacher of **Morals** <note>Latin: *Morum magistram*. A common humanist reference to Ethics.</note>, they were there at the ready, and flowed most abundantly from his tongue as he disputed and discussed. 

Even then, others believed he could rest and flourish, especially with Pluto <note>The Roman god of the underworld, often associated with wealth (Plutus). The text suggests that while he could have pursued a lucrative, comfortable career, he chose a different path.</note> being favorable; but he thought not at all. For he—to quote his words again—found in all these things no food that might feed his mind or satisfy his soul. And so he drank deeply and absorbed them, turning immediately to other things, gasping for others from which he hoped for rest and knowledge. He found that everything was indeed lesser, more uncertain, and more imperfect than its reputation had suggested, rightly [despising]...

...with them, and he had judged—I know not whether unfairly—that they deserved to be reproached as a kind of fraud. Now the **World** was flattering him, and with the most ample promises it courted his noble talent, intending to elevate him without doubt if only he had consented to its laws and made a divorce from his conscience. The splendid names of **Law and Equity** had enticed this most just man, and they made such an impression upon his talent—even though he was unwilling or less devoted to it—that they seemed to have filled the man and made him a **Doctor** not only in name, but in fact, exceeding the diligence of many. Yet, meanwhile, he was being pulled by the strings of others, and especially those of his father-in-law (truly an **Antoninus Pius** <note>Antoninus Pius (r. 138–161 AD) was a Roman Emperor renowned for his peaceful reign and integrity; the author suggests Hess's father-in-law shared these virtuous qualities.</note>), rather than using the wings of his mind upon books. 

Hence **Speyer** <note>original: "Spira". Speyer was the seat of the Imperial Chamber Court (*Reichskammergericht*), the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire, where Hess practiced law.</note> on one hand, and **Tübingen** on the other, held him as a **Jurist**; but he was one who rather abhorred the numerous deceptions lurking beneath the leaf of **Justice** than valued the benefits that flow from it to the downtrodden. Indeed, he judged it hopeless that anything better could be provided to men or more sufficiently acquired by his own talent. Thus, he was by no means hostile toward **Jurisprudence**, which is the most equitable...

# THE LIFE OF TOBIAS HESS
## HE ABANDONS THE LAW FOR MEDICINE

...and taught most holy things; but being alienated from human litigations and the sordid handling of them, as soon as he was able, he thereafter abstained from the law courts.

From his earliest years, he was already inclined toward the treatment and healing of the human body, as well as the observation of Nature’s riches—an interest to which, as he used to say, his mother’s domestic pharmacy <term>Pharmacopoeia: a collection of medicinal recipes; here referring to his mother's practical knowledge of home remedies</term> had first invited him. Therefore, while he was still subject to the demands of others, he secretly courted Medicine. It seemed to him that he derived far more pleasure from a herb or a flower painted by the very fingers of God than from any of the titles or headings of the law.

As soon as he became his own master and was, as it were, secure in his own sustenance, he embraced Medicine more fully and practiced it more frequently. Finally, when he himself was tortured by a disease so savage that it was nearly unconquerable by physicians, he united himself to the craft in earnest. He diligently studied both the great "Book of God" <note>Hess, following a common Renaissance theme, likely refers to the "Book of Nature" as a divine revelation alongside the Holy Scriptures.</note> and the smaller volumes written by those skilled in the medical arts. 

Thus, the appearance of his household was transformed: **Bartolus** and **Baldus** <note>Bartolus de Saxoferrato and Baldus de Ubaldis were the towering authorities of medieval Roman law; their names symbolize the legal career Hess was leaving behind.</note> were told "farewell" <note>original: "vale"</note>; **Hippocrates** and **Galen** <note>The primary authorities of ancient Greek medicine, representing the medical career he was now embracing.</note> were greeted with "hail" <note>original: "salve"</note>. Yet, he admitted these authors as a free man sworn to no master <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace's sentiment of intellectual independence: "not bound to swear to the words of any master."</note>, ensuring they would not rule over him or demand that his ethereal mind be enslaved. And so, when the more refined...

...so that the "purple-clad" Doctors <note>original: *purpurati Doctores*; a reference to the traditional academic elite of the medical faculties who wore scarlet or purple robes</note> might be more complete, he added Paracelsus, a man less easily disgusted when the harmony of Nature is absent, a skeptic, more patient in labor and experiments, and finally, more persistently curious in investigating the composition of matter. He would have gladly made these men friends and associates to the modest wisdom of Severinus the Dane <note>Petrus Severinus (1542–1602) was a Danish physician famous for his attempt to reconcile the traditional teachings of Galen with the new chemical medicine of Paracelsus.</note>, if only the devotees of either faction had permitted it. 

Indeed, he believed he owed it to his own freedom—which was maintained at no one else’s expense—that, far from common bickering and the rashness of poorly informed judgment, he might observe with his own eyes and touch with his hands the methods and precepts of the Paeonian art <term>Paeonian art: the art of medicine or healing, named after Paeon, the physician of the gods in Greek mythology</term> on the one hand, and on the other, its examination (or more accurately, its practical trial). In this way, he could contemplate the "divine letters" <note>Hess refers to the "signatures" or signs God imprinted on plants and minerals to indicate their healing properties.</note> imprinted here and there upon created things through the most delightful study of anatomy. This was the duty of a Philosopher who possesses such intellectual wealth: that, as far as the industry of a single man could reach, a truer and more certain light might be shed upon a matter so desperately necessary for the frail and afflicted body of man.

Thus our Hess held both Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–216 AD), the ancient authority whose humoral theory dominated medieval medicine.</note> and Paracelsus <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541), the Swiss-German physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine, often in opposition to Galen.</note> as his friends; [the followers] of both sects...

# T. HESS

...investigators <note>Completing the word from the previous page, likely *investigatores*.</note> were not all of the same kind: in those whom—whatever it was that finally stirred their bile <note>original: *bilem movit*; a classical reference to the "black bile" of anger or melancholy</note> (for I am a narrator, not an inquisitor)—certainly Christian charity vanished from their minds more than rarely. 

Indeed, the slanders of many were more impudent than even they themselves could now take delight in, and the quibbles of the common crowd too foolish to deserve enumeration; yet these were the very things that spurred Hess on and made him more courageous. O foolish little men, virtue delights in being displeasing to you; it rises when it is pressed down. You ought to have praised him if you had wanted him weakened. He began to laugh at those empty sounds, that dull judgment, those borrowed words, and that slavish tongue. Now it pleases him to put his art to the test, now to raise up the languishing, so that health itself might be hateful to you. Truly, that he came forth publicly, he was persuaded by the pleas of others; but that he came forth so willingly, at his own expense rather than that of the sick, we owe to you—as many of you as provoked a pure mind, resting within itself, with your black and envious arrows.

And so it became easy to despise the malevolent, those creeping and hissing souls. ...as many times as he despised them in his soul. Where are you who thrust forward your "paper nobility" <note>original: *chartaceam nobilitatem*; a disparaging reference to those whose only claim to status is a written title or pedigree rather than virtuous deeds</note>? This man kept the glory of his family hidden. Where are you who feign friendship with the well-born? This man withdrew himself. Where are you who beg for the petty gifts of the Great? This man avoided lavish salaries. Where are you who lick the scraps of the wealthy? This man declined the intimacy of Princes. 

To speak the truth, hardly anyone else would have escaped the flatteries of courts, the snares of gold, and the ticklings of honors, had our Hess not been as unyielding toward the invitations of the Great as he was accessible, serene, and generous toward the petitions of anyone at all. I would testify to this myself—having read illustrious letters sent to him from various courts of Germany—were it not already a matter of common knowledge at Tübingen (and even to the "Hess-scourgers" <note>original: *Heſſo-maſtigibus*; a Greek-derived construction meaning "those who whip/scourge Hess," referring to his most dedicated academic or personal critics</note>). 

Yet he did not entirely escape the favor of those Great men; though they were unwilling to be deprived of such an exquisite investigator and judge of the arts, they chose to console themselves for his absence through messengers rather than further testing the modest constancy of his mind.

# T. HESS

...cent with such applause, he had ascended the degrees of **Natural Philosophy** <note>original: *Physicæ*; in this period, this refers to the broad study of the natural world and the laws of nature</note> and **Medicine**. Yet he considered himself to have achieved nothing, unless the very same God whom he had seen as most wise in His created works, he might also hear as most merciful in the sacred scriptures. 

For this indeed remained for a man who had surely progressed through all the sciences: that he should not forget why he had been sent into this world, what he owed to time now and to eternity soon, what food the soul required, and what the limit was for a divine mind that could scarcely be satisfied; what the fellowship <note>original: *commercium*; implying a reciprocal relationship or communication between the human and the divine</note> of God with man might be, what the purification of the divine sanctuary would be, and lest he be found unmindful of his heavenly homeland. 

Therefore, he turns himself entirely toward the **Bible** (for where could he go more safely?), with his eyes, his tongue, his memory, and his heart. His reading was not measured out or distributed by the **water-clock** <term>clepsydra: a device used to measure time by the flow of water, often used to limit the length of speeches or study sessions</term>; instead, it was eager, persistent, and fervent, intermingled now with tears and sighs, now with an unusual glimmer of joy. This is entirely fitting for a book that speaks with pure gravity, pure spirit, and inexhaustible wisdom. And now, not only does he seem worthless to himself, but the entire world—the learned and the foolish alike—seems base to him; now he prefers to pray rather than to **dispute** <note>original: *disputare*; refers to the formal academic debates common in universities of the time</note>.

...wishes: now he chooses to believe rather than to inquire; now he attempts to follow rather than to know: now he longs to experience God rather than to describe Him.

Indeed, the frequent change of life—which is considered a vice by many—was for this man always glorious. It was unexpected, certainly, and if you look at the customs of these times, it appeared retrograde <note>original: *retrograda*; meaning moving backward or declining in social status, as he moved from high-paying professions to humble study</note>, yet it was straight and most swift on the journey to Heaven. Many are met who have exchanged theological severity for political license: but he who subjected a more loose life to the rules of scripture is **Hess**. To most people, the word of God seems base, while the chatter of the heathens <note>original: *gentilium*; referring to the popular secular or classical authors of the day</note> rings sweet: but he who listens to no one except God is **Hess**. To live honorably seems proper to many: but he who enters a religious life even while being mocked by others is **Hess**. Some care for the harmony of the body and the soul: but he who declares war on his own limbs <note>original: *membris suis bellum indicat*; a reference to the spiritual discipline of the "flesh" or physical desires</note> is **Hess**. Why do we repeat ourselves? **Hess** was once a good **Jurist** <term>Jurisperitus: an expert in law or a legal counselor</term>, afterwards a better **Physician**, and now he is the best **Theologian**—not ignorant (lest you be unaware) of either the ancients or the moderns, and especially...

...of our Doctors <note>original: *nostrorum Doctorum*; likely referring to the established Lutheran theologians of the period</note>, which he—as a fortunate glutton <note>original: *heluo*; one who voraciously devours books</note> of books—studied in great number. He was not unskilled in controversies or the history of heresies, yet he was more attentive to the imitation of the Christian life than to the defense of doctrine; he was more driven toward Practice <term>Praxis: the practical application of faith through action and living, rather than just intellectual study</term> than toward abstract science.

While he acted as the priest of his own body in this most devoted manner, there began to emerge—by some impulse of the age—those who sought out the threats of ruin destined for the Antichrist and the comforts meant for the more afflicted Church. Because they cited a great deal of scripture on their own behalf, they did not entirely displease Hess, as they disturbed nothing in the fundamental foundations of the Faith. And, as often happens through human curiosity, he also attempted to observe sacred numbers to see if perhaps some secret correspondences of time, or even the harmony between the old and the New Babylon <note>original: *Novi Babylonis*; in Reformation-era writing, "New Babylon" was a common pejorative for Rome or the Papal establishment</note>, might reveal something. While this was harmless at first and shared with some of the most sincere theologians, it soon became more troublesome through the fault and thoughtlessness of younger men, since not only rhythms and figures had sketched such things most obscurely...

# T. HESSI

how unjust we were in fact toward Hess, and how we, who inquired into the most sacred exercises with unwashed hands <note>original: *illoti*; literally "unwashed," a metaphor for approaching holy matters without proper reverence or purity</note>, were far too reckless—I recognized this a little later, not without a blush of shame. I have since persuaded several others, who are less prone to clinging to slanders, of the same truth. And I recall now what he himself used to say sometimes with wit (for he possessed no bitterness <note>original: *bilem*; literally "bile," meaning he lacked a resentful or angry temperament</note>): that it was a wonder that among the Evangelicals <note>The Protestants, specifically Lutherans in this context</note> a man should suffer so much toil and trouble, who threatened evil to no one except the Antichrist, while crimes truly hated by Christ are more easily covered up. I would gladly add that it is a wonder that this one "Arithmetic," if I may call it so <note>A reference to Hess’s use of sacred mathematics and biblical numbers to predict future events</note>, could have hindered even good men from every duty of Christian charity and from showing benevolence toward an excellent man.

Thus it truly is: subservience is pleasing; flatterers and mental slaves are approved; petty letters float on the surface; and mere opinion rules the day. Whatever is free, open, and solid is sent into exile; whoever has dared to doubt, let him be declared accursed; not to swear by every word and comma <note>original: *virgulas*; referring to the rigid adherence to every tiny mark of orthodox doctrine</note> is considered seditious. ...not only <note>The Latin text begins mid-sentence with *tùm*, likely the conclusion of the phrase *non solùm... sed etiam* (not only... but also).</note>, but in every single one of his opponents, he was an evaluator of their gifts, a concealer of their flaws, a mild interpreter of their slanders, and a patient overlooker of open injury. But imagine that many evils were wished, said, and done against a statue, which felt no stir of anger: unless perhaps that is an injustice to Hess, who did not merely tolerate his **Aristarchuses** <note>original: *Aristarchos*; a reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, a famous severe critic of Homer. It is another matter—though truly beneath words—what that many-headed beast (I mean the literary and working-class mob) barked out without any thought. Since he was a **mechanic** <term>Mechanicus; here meaning an engineer or inventor</term> by nature, and possessed a mind most sagacious for inventing anything, he began to practice this skill also in the construction of a **perpetual motion** <note>original: *perennis motus*; the quest for a machine that runs forever without an external energy source, a major scientific and philosophical pursuit in the 17th century.</note> machine: whether with some confidence in its success, or for the sake of examining a wonder...

# T. HESS

...[admira]ble for the sake of **Statics** <term>the branch of mechanics concerned with bodies at rest and forces in equilibrium</term>, and it is uncertain whether he was to be discovered through various machines out of a desire for equilibrium. Thus the hours, which others believe are owed to banquets under some "good title," are instead devoted to various trials of artificial motion. 

But not with your pardon, **Zoilus** <note>original: *zoile*; a traditional name for a harsh, cynical, and nitpicking critic, named after the ancient Greek critic of Homer.</note>; not without your mockery, commoner; not except with your sarcasm, you **peddler of letters** <note>original: *literi gerule*; a derogatory term for a superficial scholar or someone who carries ideas around without truly understanding them.</note>: when you barely understand the construction of a common mill that you see every day, yet you take it upon yourself to judge Hess’s most ingenious little wheels, his admirable balances, and his diversities of motion scarcely seen anywhere else. You are, of course, a more scrupulous evaluator of Hess’s spare hours the more you are an idle and barbaric prodigal waster of your own entire life. 

Truly, anyone who knows with what sharpness of invention our friend laid siege to impossibility (if it may be called so), and how much he brought forth from the storehouse of his rich genius during his search for **perpetual motion** <term>motus perpetuus; the hypothetical machine that continues to operate indefinitely without an external energy source</term>—things worthy of perpetual memory—will not unwillingly declare him a man whose equal is scarcely found, even on this one account alone.

<meta>This page continues Johann Valentin Andreae’s biographical defense of Tobias Hess. The previous page discussed Hess’s mechanical genius and inventions, such as perpetual motion.
