# IMMORTALITY

To rule—suppose there were twelve people and more—according to an exact and sacred norm, I do not know if it would have been easy for anyone; for this man, certainly, they became the **twelve labors of Hercules** <note>original: *duodecim Herculis labores*; a reference to the impossible tasks Hercules had to perform, here used to describe Hess's exhausting domestic duties</note>. This was not so much because he had to contend with the vices of this age and the various stumbling blocks of youth, as because some **Pandora** <note>In Greek mythology, Pandora opened a jar (often called a box) releasing all the evils of the world. Andreae suggests Hess’s home life was plagued by such misfortunes.</note> seemed to want to pour out all the dregs of her box into this house. 

Therefore, although he labored mightily, he was believed by many not to have sufficiently cleansed the **Augean stable** <note>original: *Augiæ stabulum*; one of Hercules' labors was to clean stables that had not been washed in thirty years, representing a massive, filthy task</note>, nor to have cut off all the regenerating heads of the **Hydra** <note>original: *hydræ*; the many-headed serpent that grew two heads back for every one cut off.</note>. But oh, how ignorant of the facts! He is presented as a good, cautious, and excellent **Paterfamilias** <term>the male head of a Roman household, having absolute legal authority over his family and servants</term> in a place where there are as many snares as there are words; as many enemies as guests; as many traitors as members of the household; where love is feigned, where respect is unwilling, where obedience is forced, and where authority is divided and discordant. 

I pray to God that none of my readers understands my words through their own experience, or possesses a house where even that all-eyed **Argus** <note>original: *Argus*; the hundred-eyed giant from Greek myth, here implying that even perfect surveillance could not manage such a chaotic household.</note> does not have eyes enough. Let those who are modest say, then, that **Tobias** <note>original: *Tobiam*; a reference to the biblical figure from the Book of Tobit who became blind; Andreae is likely playing on the name "Tobias Hess."</note> grew blind; but I, who knew him to be **Lyncean** <note>original: *lynceum*; referring to Lynceus the Argonaut, famous for his superhumanly sharp eyesight</note>, say that by the rolling of the **Sisyphian stone** <note>original: *Sisyphij saxi*; Sisyphus was condemned to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity, only for it to roll back down; Andreae uses this to describe Hess's endless and exhausting efforts.</note> above hu-

<meta>man [affairs].</meta>

<summary>Andreae defends Tobias Hess’s management of his household, comparing his domestic struggles to the labors of Hercules and the endless toil of Sisyphus. He argues that Hess was a noble head of a family despite being surrounded by discord, betrayal, and misfortune.</summary>

# TOBIAS HESS

...domestic forces, I will boldly affirm.

I will not stir this **Camarina** <note>original: *Camarina*; a proverb meaning a dangerous or messy situation. Draining the marsh of Camarina supposedly caused a plague, so the phrase means "to leave well enough alone" or to stop dwelling on a painful subject.</note> any further; let us rather delight in the virtues of Hess. He, so that he might truly present us with a **Christian Hercules** <note>original: *Herculem Christianum*; a favorite metaphor of the author, depicting the pious scholar as a hero performing spiritual labors for the sake of the Reformation and society.</note>, after he had conquered various monsters and dragged out the **Cerberus of calumny** <note>original: *calumniæ cerberum*; referring to the three-headed dog of the underworld, here representing the biting and relentless nature of slander that Hess faced.</note>, even went to support the heavens. For a long time now, the world—angered by the crimes of men and the weight of vices—has been threatening to fall into ruin, were it not for certain pious men who keep God here among us. 

Hess is one of these men. What others command, he himself does; what others praise, he himself displays; what others seek in an example, he himself provides. To him, it is a work, while to others it is merely a wish. Others have **piety** <term>original: *pietas*; a central concept in this text referring to active, lived devotion to God rather than just formal religious ritual</term> in their mouths, but he has it in his heart; for others it is on the tongue, for him it is in his deeds; for others it is in **Knowledge** <term>original: *Scientia*; here referring to theoretical or academic learning</term>, for him it is in **practice** <term>original: *praxis*; the practical application of faith and knowledge through action</term>. 

People like to worship God, but they dislike being mocked for it; he, however, rejoices in being made the laughingstock of the wicked. Others find it helpful to pray briefly, but he delights in being exhausted by prayers. What for others is merely reading or a duty, for him is an exercise of piety. He rises at dawn for prayers and resounding hymns; he eats with prayers and hymns; the night also resounds with prayers and hymns...

<meta>catchword: ...resounds,</meta>

<summary>Andreae concludes his discussion of Hess's domestic struggles and shifts to a glowing eulogy of his spiritual life, portraying Hess as a "Christian Hercules" whose private devotion and endurance of mockery help sustain the world.</summary>

# IMMORTALITY

...governs; he leads the way, interprets the sacred rites, instills **piety** <term>original: *pietas*; active, lived devotion</term>, and strikes hard at stubborn ears with fatherly warnings and severe threats. You, O God, have numbered the groans of your servant; you heard his voice when he cried out to you with his whole heart; you approved the zeal and fervor with which he sought your glory; you encouraged him when he boldly provoked the world; you received him when he renounced himself.

Amidst these things, **Impiety** roared; and not daring to openly condemn the most holy efforts of the man, she began to sting from a distance and hurl taunts. Hess was called superstitious, obsessive, eccentric, and delusional. As the lies grew, he was mocked as the **Prince of Utopia** <note>original: *Utopiensis Princeps*; a reference to Thomas More’s *Utopia*, used here to mock Hess's visions for a reformed, ideal Christian society as impossible fantasies.</note>, an interpreter of dreams, and a foreteller of the future. Those who were his closer friends, and loved as brothers, were labeled a **fanatic league** <note>original: *fanatica liga*; a derogatory term for Hess's circle of reformers, who were often accused of being part of a secret or radical conspiracy.</note>, a secret conspiracy, and a gathering of dark schemers. They were pierced sometimes with cruel and sometimes with ridiculous spears of wit; and the more monstrous a person was in their lying, the more they seemed to have truly "struck" Hess. Most Just God! That the venom...

<meta>This page continues Johann Valentin Andreae's defense of Tobias Hess against his critics. The previous page ended with a mention of the venomous lies spread by his detractors. This page concludes the thought, arguing that Hess’s "superstition" and "fanaticism" were actually marks of true Christian devotion in a corrupt age.</meta>

# T. HESS

...[dialects] and common tongues, so that solid and evident **piety** <term>original: *pietas*; lived devotion</term> becomes suspicious even to men who are neither impious nor illiterate, and so that a good man would rather use the bleary eyes of a most foul schemer <note>original: *tenebrionis*; literally a "creature of the shadows" or a shady dealer</note> than his own eyes! Oh, if only we were all "superstitious" in this way, in this age of **Epicurus**! <note>Epicurus was an ancient Greek philosopher whose name was often used in this period to criticize those seen as materialists or atheists who lived only for pleasure.</note> And if only we were "regulars" <note>meaning those following a strict religious rule or discipline</note> under Christ in this licentious age! Or unique in piety amidst such a sewage-pool of the wicked! Or "fantastical" in spiritual matters during this corporeal and coarse time! 

Oh, let us love as he did! Having cast aside the feathered crests of titles and the swelling pride of honors, let us be called brothers—why do you laugh, **Momus**? <note>Momus was the Greek god of mockery and harsh criticism, frequently used to represent those who find fault in everything.</note> Let us conspire together in the service of Christ, in contempt for the world, in mutual edification, and in friendly service to one another. Let us be grafted into a Kingdom that is not **Utopian**, but of Christ—a kingdom nearly invisible in this old age of the world. Let us threaten the impious earth with evils to come, and let us—alas, unwillingly—foretell the decline of our Fatherland! 

Thus we lived—and though you may burst with rage—with God as our witness, we lived most simply, most innocently, and most joyfully. And would that I might hope for a friend of such character for the rest of my life; I would not value all the riches of the world more highly than him!

The faith of a most devoted servant had to be examined under the more severe countenance of God.

<meta>This page continues Johann Valentin Andreae’s eulogy for Tobias Hess. The previous page established Hess as a figure of Job-like patience, suffering physical ailments and social persecution while remaining a pillar of strength for his community in Tübingen.</meta>

# TOBIAS HESS

**Hess** was never seen to be impatient; he was never heard to doubt his God, nor was he ever caught wavering in faith or hope. You might have said that health itself had conspired against him, along with the treachery of his servants, the insults of the spiteful, the judgments of the reckless, the gullibility of the weak-minded, the long passage of time, and even the exhaustion of his friends.

Though bedridden, he was sound of mind; joyful within a body that appeared like a corpse; and while enduring his own torments, he diminished the sadness of others. While he himself was oppressed by burdens, he drove away the illnesses of others; and though he was the "guardian of a small bed," <note>original: *lectuli custos*; a poetic way of saying he was confined to his sickbed.</note> he nonetheless kept a large household in order. He always sent away those who came to console him feeling consoled themselves—or rather, feeling stunned and put to shame. For he possessed more days of the most heroic endurance than they possessed words of comfort, and he displayed a greater readiness to endure than they did to merely witness his suffering.

And why shouldn't they be amazed? Here was a man who was all but bloodless and whose "brain was dry," <note>In the medical theories of the time, a "dry brain" was associated with extreme exhaustion or the depletion of vital fluids, yet Hess’s intellect remained sharp.</note> yet he contended with an indomitable spirit against those full of vigor, in eloquence against the weighers of petty words, and in memory against those who study by candlelight without ceasing.

<vocab>Hess, patience, faith, consolation, endurance, night-study</vocab>

...[ora]tors, <note>The word fragment "toribus" likely completes a word from the previous page such as *excutitoribus* (investigators) or *contionatoribus* (preachers).</note> in his contempt for wealth he contended with Kings; in his abstinence, with beggars; in his humility, with the lowliest; and in his readiness to serve, with the slave. Since all books and all subjects were ready at hand for him, not just the chapters of Holy Scripture, but the very pages and entire books (I call God to witness) could be recited perfectly <note>original: *ad unguiculum*; literally "to the fingernail," a Roman idiom for something finished with extreme precision.</note> whether he wished to do so in German or in Latin. Indeed, did he not possess within himself a living concordance <note>A concordance is an alphabetical index of words used in Scripture; Hess had memorized it so thoroughly he functioned as a human index.</note> of the Sacred Word? 

Nor was it enough for him to have applied himself to a single Translation, unless he had also explored—as he judged it necessary—the various gifts of the Spirit found in different interpreters. He was as demanding an overseer of his own memory as he was gifted with a faculty almost beyond the human lot, which remained admirable even during his frequent headaches. 

One could only listen with amazement to a man who, to all appearances, seemed a despiser of all worldly learning <term>eruditio</term>, <note>In this context, *eruditio* refers to formal, often prideful academic scholarship as opposed to spiritual wisdom.</note> yet was so well-versed in all forms of scholarship that, wherever he was tested, he seemed to have devoted himself diligently to that very subject. I believe that so few people knew of this because he so rarely and unwillingly—and then only by his closest friends <term>familiarissimi</term>—was drawn into literary disputes; and since...

### T. HESS

...in public he abstained from every haughtiness of judging; yet in private among his friends, he did not withhold his precise opinion on many matters.

Now I ought to name those friends who were accustomed to being taught by him, or to debating with him, or to caring for him and softening his wretched life with both profit and pleasure. However, I rightly refrain from naming them; for it is clear that they wish to be comrades of the mockeries <note>original: *ludibriorum*; this refers to the public ridicule, social stigma, or "games" of slander that Hess faced during his life due to his controversial views.</note> which have thus far tried his most innocent heart, and which perhaps will not spare even his sacred ashes hereafter. 

For such was the nature of Hess’s social conduct <term>conversatio</term> <note>In the 17th century, *conversatio* referred not just to talking, but to one’s entire manner of living and interacting within a community.</note> that while all good things and a virtual exemplar of virtues were visible in Hess himself, all manner of evil and most hateful reports were spread abroad—things that wounded not only the ears but even the well-being of his friends. These were sometimes spread with such bitterness that, even though it was almost always evident they were lies, they forced the more timid friends to grow faint, the more impatient to flee, and the more reckless to believe the rumors. Nevertheless, there were those from every walk of life, of no common...

->IMMORTALITY.<-

...matters, but most distinguished men, who preferred to admire, cherish, defend, and, as far as possible, imitate the sincere, certain, and proven piety of Hess, as well as his fellowship of virtues. They chose to relieve his needs rather than to neglect the debt of Christian charity <term>Christianæ charitatis</term> and brotherly love because of the most shameless lies. With what sort of conscience, with what excessive arrogance, and with what unrestrained chatter this displeased so many, it is our part to remain silent; it is for them to consider how they will excuse their poisonous credulity based on the flimsiest rumors <note>original: *rumusculis*; a diminutive form of "rumor," implying petty or insignificant gossip.</note> before the tribunal of God, when the Just Man shall stand with great confidence in the sight of those who oppressed him <note>This is a biblical allusion to the Book of the Wisdom of Solomon 5:1, often cited in funeral oratory to describe the ultimate vindication of the persecuted.</note>.

At last, however, he began to feel the weariness in his shoulders, and he started to entreat the partners of his most turbulent household management <term>Regimen</term> <note>The Latin *regimen* refers here to the administration or "rule" of his domestic and family affairs, which the author implies was a chaotic or heavy burden for Hess.</note>. To these, if I—who for a long time have been the inspector of all his "baggage" <note>original: *sarcinarum*; used metaphorically here to refer to Hess’s heavy responsibilities, his collection of papers, or the "weight" of his life’s work.</note>—were to add my own testimony, this writing of mine would become an invective...

->T. HESS<-

[descri]ption, and not a description of the daily dying of Hess. Let us rather pray well for those who, with tireless generosity, cherished, nourished, and soothed this body—scarred as it was by divine rods <note>original: *divinis ferulis*; a metaphor for the suffering or illness sent by God as a form of discipline or testing</note>. They did this not so much for their own present benefit—though they were present at the glorious struggles of a Christian soldier, heard a man undoubtedly inhabited by God, and contemplated that strength of faith in God’s furnace and that marvelous sweetness felt even under the flames—but rather for that future, inviolable reward <note>original: *fœnore*; literally "interest" on a loan, implying that God will pay back those who invested in Hess's care</note> which God, by a faithful promise, has decreed for the supporters of His servant. 

There will be a time, I believe, when it will not be shameful to have done good for Hess, nor glorious to have harassed him; it will be when that intolerable rigor of certain men of letters <note>original: *literatorum*; referring to the academic or theological critics who attacked Hess's unconventional ideas</note> will decrease, and the Christian kindness of spiritual men will increase.

Hess, always unconquered in mind, nevertheless gradually loses his body, which is of a lowlier origin; he breathes through his soul, yet faints in his limbs, and as his spirit grows, the flesh vanishes. O untamable spirit! Veins, arteries, muscles, blood, and vital fluids <note>original: *succus*; literally "juice," referring to the bodily humors believed to sustain life</note> fail him; yet he thrives, and as if [he required] none of these organs...

### IMMORTALITY

D 3

Now the Earth demands back its own, nor does he begrudge it: but it must be shown how strongly the guest-soul can thrive in this collapsing little hut <note>original: *tuguriolo*; a diminutive of *tugurium* (shack or hut), a common philosophical metaphor for the mortal body as a temporary dwelling for the eternal soul</note>. It is apparent how harsh he was toward his body, and what an anxious nourisher he was of his soul. For the latter shines, while the former withers. He was already owed to Heaven, as if stripped of his body, or truly bound to nothing further: when he proves by his example that Heaven is everywhere where God is; and when he seems to fail himself entirely, he most devotedly invites Jesus Christ—the foundation and the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: in medieval and Renaissance medicine, this was the essential fluid believed to be the primary source of life and heat in the body</term>—by whose body and blood he would hereafter be fed, by whose innocence he would be covered, by whose truth he would be strengthened, and by whose authority he would be ruled. 

O blessed lot, when man ceases and God succeeds; when the Christian succumbs and Christ presents himself; when the Flesh gasps and the Spirit inspires! Sin, you contaminate nothing: there is one who washes with a precious liquid <note>referring to the blood of Christ</note>. Satan, you persuade nothing: there is one who refutes you with the sacred word. World, you impose nothing: there is one who convicts you of fraud. Death, you terrify nothing: there is one who pulls the mask from you. Flesh, you [achieve] nothing...

(For not only the shedding of blood is counted as testimony; but also the devout, immaculate service of the mind is a daily Martyrdom, as Jerome <note>original: *Hieronymo*; Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) was a Church Father who famously argued that living a disciplined, ascetic Christian life was a "bloodless martyrdom."</note> testifies). Thus one travels to God through the narrows of Death to the splendors <note>original: *augusta*; a play on words with *angusta* (narrows). <meta>This page continues the funeral oration for Tobias Hess, transitioning from the previous page's defiance of death to the peaceful account of his final moments. It frames his passing not as a defeat, but as a transition into eternal rest.</meta>

# IMMORTALITY.
D 4

Now therefore he prepares his departure, not conquered by Death until he was conquered by Him who is Life, and for whom he had lived until now. The Conqueror of Death has broken the arrows <note>original: *spicula*; refers to the "sting of death."</note> until now, and I believe He has taken them all away; nor does Hess yield. Now let death begin to grow sweet, and with the mask of terrors laid aside, let it show itself as it truly is for Christians: the gate of salvation. Then indeed it is a joy to depart, when it has completely subdued all flesh, and when death has removed the human supports of the flesh—or rather, its hindrances—which Hess never truly loved.

But let this very companion of all life, the Teacher <note>original: *Paedagogus*; in the ancient world, a pedagogue was a slave who escorted children to school and supervised their conduct. Here, death is seen as the one who "disciplines" the body to prepare the soul.</note> of the Flesh, the Physician of the Soul, and the noble Mystery of Christianity, grow so mild that it embraces Hess with a gentle countenance, and invites him to eternal heavenly rest through nothing but a sweet slumber. He hears the divine messenger, and having said "Farewell" to his own and "Hail, Jesus," he peacefully composes himself for a sleep that no man can disrupt; he placidly fades away; he holily falls asleep.

It was the 24th day of November in the year 1614, when Hess, leaving the unclean world <note>original: *Mundum immundum*; a clever Latin pun where *mundus* means both "the world" and "clean." By calling it *mundum immundum*, the author is saying "the unclean clean-place."</note>, entered the hoped-for Heaven...

<summary>This page describes the peaceful death of Tobias Hess on November 24, 1614, portraying death not as a terror but as a "gate of salvation" and a "sweet slumber" leading to heaven.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the eulogy for Tobias Hess, moving from the specific moment of his death to the collective grief felt by the community and the distribution of his spiritual and physical legacy. It transitions from his entrance into heaven to the void he leaves behind in Germany and the University of Tübingen.</meta>

# TOBIAS HESS
<header>T. HESS</header>

It is truly a day notable for many reasons, and for many more, it is a day of mourning. For on this day, Earth lost its guest, Germany its star, Tübingen its ornament, Princes their delight, Nature its interpreter, Piety its torch, the sick their help, Erudition its eye, the good their mirror, friends their gem, and the wicked their target <note>original: *scopum*; implies he was a target for the criticism or malice of the "wicked" due to his virtues.</note>. But above all these, the Christian body <note>referring to the *Corpus Christianum*, the collective community of the Church</note> lost a noble and notable member. Or rather, it has returned such a rare deposit of Christ to its rightful Owner in such a way that the Church now longs for him in his absence more than it did when he was present; it seems to itself—though in vain—to be less able to go on without him. *He now rests in peace—this was once his personal motto—and he magnifies God <note>original: *Elohim*; Hebrew term for God often used in Hess's circles</note> beyond the borders of Israel.*

Meanwhile, he gladly divides what was his among his heirs: he returns, commends, and leaves his soul to heaven, his body to the earth, his example to the good, shame to the wicked, his fame to the world, and his testimony to posterity. Whatever remaining honors were due to Hess’s mortal remains <note>original: *exuviis*; literally "spoils" or "cast-off skins," a poetic way to refer to the body after the soul has departed</note>, Tübingen paid them most generously.

[ *November 27th, the first Sunday of Advent* <note>The start of the liturgical year in the Christian calendar.</note> ]

The final funeral praises were delivered by the Reverend and Distinguished Matthias Hafenreffer, the pious (he holds this epithet from Hess himself).

<summary>This page reflects on the immense loss felt by the intellectual and religious community upon the death of Tobias Hess, detailing his legacy and his funeral held in Tübingen during Advent.</summary>
<keywords>Tobias Hess, Tübingen, Matthias Hafenreffer, funeral oration, legacy, Advent, Elohim, Germany</keywords>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>IMMORTALITY</header>

and completed it with the customary sacred eloquence. The funeral procession? All the witnesses of Hess’s innocence <note>original: *Hessianae innocentiae*; referring to the personal character of Tobias Hess rather than the region of Hesse.</note> flocked together in vast numbers; tears (even if some were perhaps forced) flowed truly and most abundantly. The burial? It was given among his own people, and in the place he had desired. The epitaph? It shall stand, if not in marble, then in the hearts and on the tongues of all the best people. The prayer? All, all, every good person whispered it silently: *To the best Hess, to the well-deserving Hess, to the unfairly afflicted Hess: rest, reward, and a most joyful resurrection!*

And what of me? I rejoice, but with tears; I offer congratulations, but with a groan; and I find gladness in the midst of my deepest grief. Thus do I weep for my Hess, who is delighted to be set free; thus do I miss my friend, even while it pleases me that he has been snatched away <note>from the trials of life</note>; thus do I lose a brother, yet I feel as though I have gained him for myself. It may befit others to wail; but let applause adorn Hess, and let us follow his ascending, blessed soul with shouts of joy. Why? When he was suffering, he forbade us to grieve with him; now that he is liberated and triumphant, should he have to endure a dirge <note>original: *lessum*; a song of lamentation or a funeral wail.</note>? I am not unaware of what my country, and what I myself, am losing; but—

it is more fitting to favor the advantages of a most 
dear friend than to calculate my own losses. 
I grant this to Hess alone: that I laugh 
despite my loss. Let it be ill for me, so long as it is 
well for him; I shall suffer, so long as he 
emerges free from his bonds. O Hess, whether 
I call you Father, or Brother, or Teacher, or 
Friend, or Companion, it is all the same, 
and you alone have earned so many names from 
me; now you impose upon me as many titles <note>titles of mourning or bereavement</note> 
as those have who are deprived of such loved 
ones. Is this that day—as you recently 
wrote to me with a weak hand—of your 
soul returning into its rest? Truly, 
truly you have returned, after you have 
laboriously traversed the malice of Satan, 
the treachery of the World, the trouble of the 
Flesh, and the ferocity of Death. Now you 
sleep securely, and you do not fear the 
torments of the body, the deceits of acquaintances, 
the threats of poverty, the terrors of 
infamy, the teeth of the wicked, the snares 
of the crafty, the laughter of the superficial <note>original: *sciolorum*; those who possess a mere smattering of knowledge without true depth or wisdom.</note>, 
or the petty excuses of the greedy. I grieve 
indeed for your sake, brother, my Hess; you 
were pleasant to me beyond measure, so that 
your love was worth more to me than the love 
of women <note>A biblical allusion to David’s lament for Jonathan in 2 Samuel 1:26.</note>.

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page concludes the eulogy for Tobias Hess, following the author’s reflections on the peace Hess has found in death. The author now shifts from mourning the loss to addressing the immortal legacy of his friend and his own inadequacy in honoring such a figure.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
# IMMORTALITY.
D 6

What can I give back to you, my brother? How shall I settle my debt to you? With praise? You are beyond all eulogies. Should I offer a defense? You are beyond all accusations. Should I describe you for posterity? You are immortal. Whatever I do, any small share of glory <note>original: *gloriola*; a modest or slight glory.</note> belongs to me for having had you as a friend; it is now a matter of pride for me to profess the friendship of a *most learned, most unconquered, most sincere, and most religious man.* Therefore, I remain silent, and as is done in sacred rites, I testify to my awe through silence, and to the infinite nature of your genius through my faltering speech. 

At the same time, I become a suppliant to God, praying that even in death you may finally rest from the malice <note>original: *livore*; literally "blueness" or "bruising," used here to mean the "envy" or "spite" of critics.</note> of the wicked; that you may finally be blessed and formidable to the ungodly, and triumphant and remarkable to those who would imitate you. Finally, having been described thus truly and sincerely by a friend, may you leave the memory of yourself and a sense of longing to all good men, so that you might also, as soon as possible, embrace me—joyous to joyous, free to free—among the blessed joys of Jesus Christ.

->Farewell!<-

<vocab>brother, debt, praise, posterity, immortal, friendship, silence, God, Jesus Christ, Farewell</vocab>

My Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician traditionally regarded as the "Father of Medicine."</note> once lived—oh, he was my Machaon, <note>In Greek mythology, Machaon was the son of Asclepius and a highly skilled healer for the Greek army at Troy.</note>
That man HESS, who was a God to the sick through his skill.
Grief murmurs here in opposition: Either he should never 
Have been born, or he should not have died so soon.
But reason speaks: HESS was like Job, <note>A reference to the biblical figure known for enduring extreme suffering with patience.</note>
Unless perhaps he even endured more crosses; <note>A common metaphor for heavy trials or religious persecution.</note>
But because the degenerate world, trampling upon virtues,
Bestows rewards on the undeserving, rather than the worthy,
God called the veteran <note>original: *emeritum*; a term for a soldier who has completed his service and is entitled to rest.</note> away: and now he is a citizen of Olympus, <note>A classical poetic allusion to Heaven.</note>
Happy now, he who was the unhappy HESS before.

## The Good Memory
### of Johannes Schermar.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'M' with leafy flourishes inside a square border.</image-desc>Great is the glory of your city of Ulm, <note>original: "Ulmensis"; referring to the Free Imperial City of Ulm, a major economic and political center in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> Great Senators, <note>The term "Senators" refers to the members of the city's ruling Council (the *Magistrat*).</note> most excellent Citizens, by which—among many of the most celebrated cities of Germany—it stands out above many others in the sincerity of its Religion, the extent of its domain, the healthfulness of its air, the fertility of its soil, the convenience of its commerce, the splendor of its buildings, and the strength and fortification of its walls. # I. THE SCHERMAR FAMILY

[your ancestors’] most distinguished Wisdom and Fortitude, <note>Continuing the sentence from page 89: "...it is [the benefit] of your ancestors' most distinguished Wisdom and Fortitude..."</note> which have excelled to such an extent in them that if anyone were able to enumerate their wholesome Councils and praiseworthy Deeds, he would open a field at once vast and most delightful—both for the praise of those same ancestors and your City, and for the shaping of an ideal Republic. <note>original: "Rempublicam"; here referring to the "commonwealth" or the well-ordered governance of the city-state of Ulm.</note>

Therefore, the greater the Merits of your Ancestors, the greater your joy must be: you who appear to be the heirs of these virtues and this fame. So too should the congratulations of all of us be more ready—we who enjoy the happiness of your City, which contributes to the greater flowering, fame, and peace of Swabia, <note>original: "Sveviæ"; the historic region in southwestern Germany.</note> our Fatherland. If we seem as worthy to you for singing these deserved praises as you are judged by us most worthy of the commendations of all good men, then let us most easily be joined—you in receiving and we in delivering our judgments, which shall forever be these: *There is scarcely anything that can be added further to your Dignity; nor indeed scarcely anything that can be added to our applause.*

# MEMORY
<meta>catchword: exami-</meta>

Therefore, you may expect a public proclamation <note>original: "praeconium"; a formal public announcement or heraldic praise</note> of your City, or even of each of you individually, from some famous and most learned man. I, however—so that it might be clearly evident how much even we, the least among you, yearn to reveal our affection toward you—have considered this: if I were to take upon myself the task of delivering a eulogy <note>original: "encomium"; a formal speech of high praise</note> for at least one person from so great a number of those deserving praise, it would be sufficient to reveal my devotion. 

Yet, when I realized how limited my own talent was to produce words worthy of such a praiseworthy life, I was almost ready to withdraw, had I not been held back on both sides: here, by the bond of duties to which I am bound to you; and there, by the allure of that refined kindness <note>original: "humanitatis"; referring to the combination of culture, compassion, and polished character</note> with which you so excellently temper your own brilliance.

And so, as I prepare to write to you concerning a certain most virtuous and excellent Citizen of your Fatherland—though I know that title applies to many others as well, and I congratulate Ulm <note>the city of Ulm in Swabia</note> on that very account—I believe you easily sense that there is hardly anyone in this age to whom, whether considering his years, or the labors he has devoted to the Fatherland, or the gathering of his most rare gifts... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

## I. SCHERMARIUS

...we should consider whether eternal Memory <note>original: "Memoria"</note> is owed by grateful citizens; and yet, by the world of scholarship <note>original: "bonis literis"; literally "good letters," referring to the humanities and the academic community</note>, a public tribute <note>original: "elogium"</note> is owed by even greater Merit than to your celebrated and truly Noble man, Master *Johannes Schermarius*, distinguished Doctor of Both Laws <term>Doctor of Both Laws: A person who has earned a doctorate in both Civil (Roman) and Canon (Church) law, abbreviated as V.I.D. or J.U.D.</term>. Regarding what he might hope for in terms of being remembered, those who are not ignorant of the high regard and respect in which you hold him, and the reverence with which you honor him, can easily imagine it. 

Truly, the duty of providing a formal eulogy—which in my opinion is conferred among the learned from one to another as something requiring excellent talent—has until now remained hidden. This is because, although such a great man is more accustomed to performing praiseworthy deeds than to hearing praise, and has most modestly suppressed the many tributes written about him, the readiness of all good men to honor him has always been apparent. Indeed, this affection has burst forth from several people into true and well-deserved praise.

Therefore I, the lowliest of all those who might be able to serve him, having been moved by his kind conversation and the sight of so many outstanding virtues, toward...

<page-num>93</page-num>

<header>MEMORIAL</header>

...admiration, veneration, and love for him, that I have until now burned with such a desire to express my feelings. Consequently, I have set aside not only my unworthiness—by which I might be an embarrassment to such a great man—but also the "coldness of my talent" <note>original: "ingenij frigiditate"; a common self-deprecating trope where the author claims to lack the "fire" of true inspiration or genius</note>. I have now dared to step forward to sing the praises of a man so deeply loved by me. I do this before you, most distinguished and excellent men, who are already so filled with love for him that I fear I may omit the very qualities you most admire in him, or perhaps even defile them with my "barbarous tongue" <note>original: "barbarâ meâ linguâ"; another humility trope where the speaker suggests his Latin is unpolished or unsophisticated compared to the subject's greatness</note>. 

You will consider, however, that just as most of us succumb to love, I have decided to write here not because it will add any further honor to this most celebrated man, but because it will most clearly reveal my own mind and my judgment of him. I proceed with all the greater confidence because I hope that the frequent repetition of his name alone might "season" <note>original: "condire"; the author uses a culinary metaphor, suggesting Schermar's name acts as salt to make a bland speech palatable</note> even the most tasteless oration—especially among those who consider *who* I am writing about, rather than *how* I am writing.

->Wise men of old once called the Best Citizen<-

# I. THE SCHERMARS

...said, is he who is Honorable, useful, and pleasant to his Fatherland. <note>The author is completing the definition of the "Best Citizen" mentioned on the previous page: "Wise men of old once called the Best Citizen [the one] who is..."</note> Which of these qualities shall I apply first to our Schermar, **Most Distinguished Senators**? Everywhere there is a great harvest of material and examples. Yet I shall speak of the *Honorable* first: for is that not the primary consideration? To be the son of those parents who have been honorable in **Ulm** for several centuries now: on the father’s side, the **Schermars**; on the mother’s side, the **Roths**: men ennobled by their love for the Fatherland and by the many benefits they bestowed upon it, and who are adorned by the eternal testimony of their merits. 

Indeed, they are the ones—and among them are those—through whom and with whom **Ulm** ascended to such a height of fame. Just as the city now confers ornament upon the birthright of its citizens, it receives as much honor back from the memory of several most noble families when it remembers those <term>Elders</term><note>original: "Seniores"; senior members of the city hierarchy</note>, <term>Consuls</term><note>original: "Consules"; in the context of an Imperial Free City like Ulm, this refers to the Mayors or *Bürgermeister*</note>, <term>Senators</term>, and commanders of armies found among the **Schermars**, **Roths**, **Ehingers**, **Besserers**, **Krafts**, and **Liebers** (who [were] the grandfathers and grandmothers of our Schermar...

God willed him to see this light <note>A poetic way of saying "to be born."</note> in the year 1525, on the 11th day of September, almost exactly at the dawn of the saving Gospel <note>original: "Salutaris Evangelij"; this refers to the early years of the Protestant Reformation</note>, of the finest arts in **Germany**, and of a Christian world seemingly renewed. Thus, as often as we look upon him, he can serve as a mark and a sign to us of German—indeed, of Christian—prosperity.

Furthermore, so that he might not fall away from such great virtue in his parents, he first drew the seeds of true **Piety**, of refined **Morals**, and of **Ancestral Honor** from his beloved parents, as well as the first principles of Latin literature. Indeed, in **Pomerania**, how could he not have brought honor to his Fatherland? ->## MEMORY.<-

...to his Fatherland? Was it not well-known that their citizen was celebrated in the name of true and sincere **Piety**, of a purer **Religion** <note>original: "purioris Religionis"; likely referring to the Lutheran faith of the Reformation</note> (for the confession of which his ancestors lost their splendid wealth), and indeed of all the virtues that can most greatly adorn this age? All these things could testify to the excellence of the method of educating children used in **Ulm**; namely, our man of **Ulm** was perpetually spoken of as the best among the good, the most faithful among the faithful, and the most modest among the chaste. He received such great approval from the better sort of men, and caused such a roar from Satan, that (to say nothing of his other virtues) through his observance of **Chastity** alone, he incurred the danger of his life because of a most impure woman—not unlike the holy **Joseph** of old <note>A reference to the biblical story of Joseph and Potiphar’s wife, where Joseph was nearly executed after refusing the advances of his master's wife.</note>. ...the Theologian, he decreed an honorable embassy; this man alone seemed worthy to him to be joined to the Envoys, by a truly secret and wondrous plan of God. Thus this young man (whom God intended to make great) was kindled to the imitation of a rare zeal and fervent **Piety** by the sight and conversation of <term>the Great Luther</term> <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the primary catalyst of the Protestant Reformation.</note>—a man born for the glory of **Germany** and of our century—and kindled to a love of letters by the flowering of the <term>University of Wittenberg</term> <note>Founded in 1502, this university became the center of the Protestant Reformation.</note>.

After a decade had finally passed, having been most honorably dismissed by the **Prince of Pomerania** <note>The Dukes of Pomerania were the sovereign rulers of the region where Schermar studied and worked.</note> and increased in honor by a new **embassy** <note>original: "legatio"; a diplomatic mission or official assignment.</note> to be undertaken on his journey—and (what was especially most pleasing to him) being not lightly imbued with **good letters** <note>original: "bonis literis"; referring to a deep education in classical literature, Greek, and Latin.</note>—he returned to his **Fatherland** <note>original: "Patriam"; here referring to his home city or region.</note>, where one of the citizens of the Fatherland could have welcomed him upon his return with these words:

He returns differently than others; not having shamefully squandered his **Patrimony** <note>original: "Patrimonio"; his family inheritance or wealth.</note>, but having increased it; not with his Piety lost, but increased; not diminished in the **Arts** <note>The "Liberal Arts," the standard curriculum of higher education.</note>, but increased; and now he returns as a new and unexpected **Nursling of the Muses** <term>Musarum Alumnus</term> <note>original: "Musarum Alumnus"; a classical metaphor for a scholar or poet nurtured by the Muses.</note>. Rejoice, parents, who receive a son whom other parents might set forth as a **mirror** <note>original: "speculum"; an ideal model to be reflected or imitated.</note> and singular example for their own sons when they send them out. Rejoice, friends, who from such a great proof of passion can guess what is to be hoped for from him, once he applies himself to the care of the Fatherland.

But the Fatherland was not yet satisfied in this way, nor was the joy of his parents yet full,

He did not yet believe the expectations of his friends had been sufficiently satisfied. Setting out from **Ulm** for a second time, he returned to **Wittenberg**. After two years there, during the wars of **Emperor Charles V** and several **German Princes** <note>Refers to the Schmalkaldic War (1546–1547), a conflict between the Holy Roman Emperor and the Lutheran princes.</note>—at which time he was in such immediate danger that a **musket ball** <term>sclopeti glans</term> <note>original: "sclopeti glans"; literally a "ball of the musket." Firearms were still a relatively recent and terrifying development in 16th-century warfare.</note> penetrated his chamber at the very spot where he was accustomed to sit—he was driven out, nearly along with **Good Letters** <note>original: "bonis Literis"; the study of classical literature and the humanities.</note> themselves. Consequently, he planned a third journey to **Italy** for the study of **Jurisprudence** <note>original: "Jurisprudentiæ"; the scholarly study of Law.</note>, which was famous throughout the whole world at that time.

Thus he entered **Padua**; there, for two full years, he moved among the most learned men of that age, who were like the very lights of literature. He conversed with them and, in addition to **Latin**, he made the **Italian language** even more familiar to himself than his **mother tongue** <note>original: "vernaculam"; his native German.</note>. He achieved such proficiency that he was frequently employed as an interpreter in the most serious affairs.

But what then? Did he lose his religious fervor while in the **Pope’s Kingdom**? Did he bow himself down before the **Seven-Headed Beast** <note>original: "Septicipiti Bestia"; a common Protestant Reformation-era polemical reference to the Papacy, drawn from the apocalyptic imagery in the Book of Revelation.</note>? Far be such an abomination from this religious heart! Indeed, because of the **Papal idolatry** <note>original: "Idololatriam Pontificiam"; a standard Protestant critique of Roman Catholic rituals.</note> he witnessed, so...

# I. SCHERMARII #

A religion rivaling paganism <note>original: "Gentiliſmi æmulą Religio"; the author, writing from a Protestant perspective, compares Catholic rituals to ancient pagan customs.</note> became loathsome to him in every way. To convince its followers of this, frequent crimes provided so many arguments and materials that he manfully mocked the **Pope** <term>Pontificem</term> and unmasked him in such a way that the stings of conscience inflicted horrible penalties upon a certain man—who, though better instructed by Schermar, nevertheless preferred to lose his soul rather than his wealth.

When a loathing for **Padua** had seized him, he withdrew to **Siena** <note>original: "Senas"</note>, another celebrated Academy of **Italy**. However, terrified by the arms and the siege of the **Florentine Prince** <note>Cosimo I de' Medici, who conquered Siena in 1555 after a brutal siege supported by the Emperor.</note>, or rather of the **Emperor** <note>Charles V.</note>, and because there was a great scarcity of provisions there, he fled quickly from that place and sought **Rome**. This city was once the capital of the world, but now the capital of crimes. Having stayed there through the entire season of **Lent** <term>Quadrageſimale</term> <note>The forty-day period of fasting and penance preceding Easter.</note>, he saw the very fulfillment of abominations and idolatries, and even the **Prince of Iniquity** <note>A common polemical term used by Reformers to describe the Pope.</note> himself, **Pope Julius III**. He saw how at **Rome** all things are for sale and all things are permitted; he saw the horrendous worship of **Relics**. Yet, he also saw the most splendid structures and the ruins of the once greatest City, as an instruction for...

<header>
### REMEMBRANCE ###
</header>

...prudence; but full of hatred for the **Pope** <note>original: "Papæ"; following his negative experiences in Rome described on the previous page.</note>, he set out for **Perugia** <note>original: "Perusiam"; a major university city in central Italy.</note>, where he once again added something to his learning. Finally, in **June of the year 1555**, he went to **Ferrara**. There, after nine years of constant study—during which he absorbed several languages, philosophy, and **Jurisprudence** <term>Jurisprudentia</term> <note>The theory or philosophy of law.</note>—with such great labor and diligence, and by an example of the rarest effort, he received a worthy reward: the insignia of a **Doctor in Both Laws** <term>Doctor in utroque Jure</term> <note>A prestigious degree indicating mastery of both Civil Law (Roman law) and Canon Law (Church law).</note>.

Bearing these honors like a trophy, joyful and exultant, he returned to his **Homeland** by a journey that had proved most useful to him. His homeland, longing for its citizen and son because of the fame of such a title, cheered him as he arrived and welcomed him warmly as he entered. In that place, however, he did not yet believe he should rest entirely or take up the governance of his country until he had consolidated these studies and his excellent knowledge of the Law through the exercise of practical experience. For this purpose, he was present first in **1556** at **Esslingen** <note>original: "Eslingæ"; a free imperial city.</note> (staying there while in exile because of the plague), and afterwards at **Speyer** at the **Imperial Chamber** <term>Imperiali Cameræ</term> <note>The Reichskammergericht, the highest judicial court of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>. He remained there until he believed his return to his homeland would be simultaneously honorable, useful, and pleasant.

I have brought back to you, **Fathers of the City** <note>original: "Patres"; a respectful address to the city councilors or elders of Schermar’s home.</note>, your **Schermar**, safe, vigorous, flourishing, and even as a **Doctor**. Almost his entire youth was spent among **foreigners** <note>original: "Peregrinos"; referring to his years of study and travel across Europe.</note>; but it was spent in such a way that if we were to count only the years, he was away a long time; yet if we compare that time with his **Learning** <term>Eruditione</term>, it must rightly be said that he could not have traveled long enough to earn such a high title, or to acquire such great **Wisdom** <term>Sapientia</term>, **Prudence** <term>Prudentia</term>, and **Experience** <term>Experientia</term>, along with the knowledge of several languages and so detailed an account of the things he saw. 

Therefore, this **Return** <note>original: "Reditus"</note> is marvelous and full of Divine aid, whether we look at his unexpected progress, or the fact that God wished to provide for this City with such an outstanding and celebrated man, or even the dangers that sought him out, yet through which he remained untouched until this moment. I shall speak of things that may increase the glory of God and stir admiration for His help. Nine times he ran the risk of his life and held death before his eyes: once when he had imprudently boarded a small boat that was being swept out into the **Ocean** <note>original: "Oceanum"</note> by the force of the winds; next, in a most dangerous wreck of a **carriage** <note>original: "Rhedæ"; a four-wheeled traveling coach.</note>; then, by a wicked man’s lethal blow to the head...

Surely, He whom God wished to be a **Herald** <term>Præconem</term> of His Majesty was taught—by being submerged in so many great dangers and then pulled out again—never to despair of Divine aid, and never to cease expecting help from God. 

Therefore, if he has returned to you as a **Learned Man** <term>Eruditus</term>: it is to the honor of your City that it sent out such men, who were able to achieve anything through fervent diligence. A **herald of God**: it is the honor of your City to have sent out such men, who, from the very moment of their departure, had God as a companion, a leader, and a guide for their return. He has returned them to you so that, like successful **Merchants**, they have come back enriched—not with gems, spices, and various wares—but with **Prudence**, **Wisdom**, **Erudition**, **Experience** <term>Rerum usu</term> <note>Literally "the use of things," referring to practical knowledge gained through travel and observation.</note>, and the entire circle of **Virtues**. Thus, from the multitude of wise men, may the safety of your City, its Fame, Glory, and Prosperity grow, increase, and flourish.

I have now paid due tribute to the honor of your City, most distinguished Men and best Citizens; now, I must speak of how useful Schermar was to it, and how he remains so even now. I shall not at this time merely extol the fact that, after he had joined himself in the sacred bond of Matrimony to the noble and Christian matron, **Elisabeth Clamer**—the most praised widow of the late distinguished and noble man, Master Peter Oberburger, of happy memory <note>original: "f. r.", an abbreviation for "felicis recordationis."</note>—he lived with her for forty full years. Although it was not entirely sterile, for one little son, **Johannes**, was added to the heavenly choirs after his 15th month of life. Truly, the care of his instruction and education overflowed so successfully onto his two step-daughters, **Elisabeth**, of blessed memory <note>original: "b. rec.", an abbreviation for *beatae recordationis*.</note>, and **Sabina**. The former was married to **Master Paul Schermar**, the most beloved brother of this colleague of ours, and the latter to **Master Paul Roth**, a Senator—two most honored Patricians <term>Patriciis</term> <note>The hereditary ruling elite or noble families of a free imperial city.</note> who are now resting peacefully in Christ. Your City has always admired these women for their rare gifts of the mind, their love of **Religion**, their observance of **Chastity**, and their excellent knowledge of managing domestic affairs; and it still admires, looks up to, and loves **Sabina**, who is still (would that it be for as long as possible!) a survivor and a **model of Matrimonial Virtues**.

### J. SCHERMAR

...and expects just as much from posterity: on one side from **Master Johannes Schermar**, a noble Senator, blessed with many children; on the other from **Master Paul**, the Legal Counsel, **Master Johannes** the Politician, and **Master Johannes Leo**, the Theologian—the **Roth brothers**—and their sister **Elisabeth**, who was recently <insert>[on August 29, 1609]</insert> betrothed under happy auspices to the most excellent **Master Christopher Welling**, Licentiate of Laws. ### MEMORIA.

...ble interpretations, and all those things which an excellent Citizen, an excellent Patron, but above all an excellent Christian can do for the benefit of Religion and the Christian Commonwealth <term>Reipublicæ</term>, he did and carried out, so that the **Glory of God** <note>original: *Gloria Dei*</note>, the **Safety of the Fatherland** <note>original: *Salus Patriæ*</note>, the **Usefulness to One’s Neighbor**, and the **Honor of the Family** were always his supreme Laws. 

Thus it is no wonder that the counsels of such a Religious and Prudent Man were followed by the most happy outcomes from God; that the example of such a conscientious Legal Expert <term>Jurisperiti</term> <note>A legal scholar or advocate skilled in the law.</note> gained great authority for Justice; and that the imitation of such an excellent Father of a household touched many.

Let us therefore call him truly useful to his Fatherland—he who benefits Religion, who benefits the Commonwealth, and who benefits the Household <term>Oeconomiæ</term> <note>Derived from the Greek *oikonomia*, referring here to the management of family affairs and the domestic estate.</note>: and he so expended his labors that every rank of civil society <term>status Civilis</term> <note>The various social orders or "estates" that make up the community.</note> has cause to confess themselves bound to this one man, whom they might wish a long life, and whom they might long for after death: Let the **Church** <term>Ecclesia</term> call him Faithful, the **State** <term>Politia</term> call him Industrious, the **Household** call him Beneficial, his wife call him sweet, his daughters...

### OF JOHANNES SCHERMAR
<note>Johannes Schermar (1552–1630) was a prominent jurist and councilor in Nuremberg. This text appears to be part of a memorial oration or biography celebrating his long life and service.</note>

...deserved most highly, a friend to the Citizens, and generous to the Poor.

I restrain myself here from fuller praise, as I am well aware of his **Modesty** <note>original: *Modestiam*</note>, and with a silent prayer I follow those many praiseworthy things he is still to accomplish: For he lives in such a way that his whole life, and each individual day of his life, are a credit to your City; and thus we should wish for more such days, just as we desire the increase of your praises with eager prayers.

But let us finally come to that which is **Pleasant** <note>original: *Iucundum*. I will not repeat here the flower of his Youth, the strength of his more mature age, nor the cheerfulness of his **Old Age** <term>Senectutis</term>: all of which have either been told to you by your Parents, or seen by yourselves before now. 

Let us look up to that one thing: his advanced age, which he now enjoys by a rare blessing of God—an age which, after his **84th year**, is scarcely subject to any diseases, needing no cane, and hinders him from no convenience of life; that vigorous age, in which a few weeks ago

# MEMORY.

He participated in the **Welling Wedding** <note>original: *Wellingianis Nuptiis*</note> with every kind of cheerfulness and joy, to the admiration of everyone. Some say that **Old Age** <term>Senectus</term> itself is a disease; but for him, it is so far from troublesome that it has weakened none of his senses except for his *Hearing* <note>original: *Auditus*</note>, which is a little duller. Truly, his *Memory*, his *Judgment*, and the pleasantness of his *Conversation* are so excellent in him that those who are familiar with the grumpiness of old men find here something they must deservedly admire and love, making them long to be in his company forever. 

Here you may hear the *Acts*—both public and private—of a past time, I might almost say of a century; you may hear the particularly memorable course of his own life; you may hear the successions of *Ancestors* <note>original: *Majorū*</note> of the City of Ulm <note>original: *Ulmensis*</note>, all known to him. Indeed, he is the only one now remaining in Ulm who, like a certain *Janus* <note>The Roman god of beginnings and transitions, usually depicted with two faces looking simultaneously into the past and the future.</note>, looks as if into another era; he who lived among your *Fathers*, *Grandfathers*, and *Great-Grandfathers*, and was their friend. What, I ask, can be more pleasant to you than to have a living *Chronicle* of your ancestors among you

### J. SCHERMARII

...and walk vigorously? Your **Ulm** <note>original: *Ulma vestra*</note> truly rejoices when it gazes upon him, still healthy and full of life, who has witnessed several of its changing fortunes <note>The Latin *vicissitudines* refers to the various shifts in luck or political stability the city experienced.</note>. Your illustrious **Senate** <term>Senatus</term> rejoices that he survives—he who was present at many thousands of the wisest counsels of the Fatherland. All the **Citizens** rejoice that this torch still shines before them, which has been their greatest guide in **humanity** <term>humanitas</term> and **integrity** <term>probitas</term> until now. His relatives rejoice in such an honorable kinsman; his **daughter** rejoices in such a sweet and good father; his **grandchildren** rejoice in a most indulgent and generous grandfather. Indeed, everyone—even **strangers** <term>Peregrini</term>—rejoices in his conversation and company; they always take something away from him that leaves them with an eternal memory of this excellent, most pleasant, and most devout old man.

And so I, too, inspired by his outstanding **virtues**, did not know how else to express my affection or show in what high regard I hold such a rare example of human modesty. Fearing it might be burdensome for him to be praised by me—someone perhaps ill-suited for the task—his praises...

# MEMORY.

I have linked his honors with the praises of your City and your ancestors in such a way that from him, glory and fame might reach them and you; and from the city, glory might reach them and you alike. For us, who admire your city, this single example makes it clear what kind of men **Ulm** <note>original: *Ulma*</note> once produced, whom she still nurtures today, and what kind she may dare to hope for in the future.

As for **Schermarius**, a man who is an honor, a benefit, and a joy to his Fatherland, I wish to bid him farewell in this way: while I hope and even pray that many years of a most honorable, Christian, and praiseworthy life may yet be added to him by God’s mercy, I have no doubt that many things remain for him to accomplish honorably before his blessed departure from this life. These deeds will eventually require a new herald, one more eloquent than myself. Meanwhile, if my own meager abilities <term>Tenuitas</term> <note>A common rhetorical device where the author downplays their own talent to further elevate the subject they are praising.</note> can contribute anything to his reputation among those who do not know him, I frankly confess that I have never more willingly or promptly devoted myself to the praise of any man.

To you, most noble **Senators** <term>Senatores</term>, who know all these things and even more...

# THE FIRE
## OF VAIHINGEN
### The First

->On the Kalends of November in the Year 1617,<- <note>original: "Ad Calendas Novembris"; the Kalends refers to the first day of the month, November 1st.</note>
->raging for 10 hours,<-
->a brief and true account.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut printers' ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage, floral motifs, and stylized leaf patterns.</image-desc>

->On the **E**ve of the **S**aints, **V**aihingen **B**urns.<- <note>original: "sanCtorVM Veſperâ arDet VaIhInga." This is a chronogram, a popular 17th-century word puzzle where specific letters (C, V, M, V, D, V, I, I) are read as Roman numerals. When added together (100+5+1000+5+500+5+1+1), they sum to the year 1617. The "Eve of the Saints" refers to October 31st, the night the fire began.</note>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication (concluded on the previous page) to the main narrative of the work. Johann Valentin Andreae begins his eyewitness account of the Great Fire of Vaihingen, which occurred in 1618.</meta>

-># FIRE<-

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial letter 'I' containing scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

**A horrible fire**, which a Zealous God <note>original: "Deus Zelotes"; a reference to the biblical "Jealous God" who punishes iniquity but also calls for repentance.</note>, provoked by the sins of humanity, undoubtedly sent upon **Vaihingen**—one of the chief cities of **Württemberg**, situated on the river **Enz** between **Pforzheim** and **Bietigheim** in a fertile and beautiful location—has by now filled the ears of many people far and wide with inconsistent reports. For the news has been received by many as something greater than it was, and by very many as something less, often mixed with fabrications; it has been understood and remembered according to how it reached the ears of those ready to believe it. 

But I, because I was an eyewitness to every detail, even the smallest, and was in some small way a part of these events, felt I should grant my friends' request to receive from me a true account of this most mournful matter, however brief it may be. If the purpose of this account is to stir the compassion of good people today, and to pass down to future generations a *Christian* memory of this divine rod <note>The "divine rod" (virga divina) is a common historical metaphor for God’s discipline or punishment intended to correct a community.</note>, that is enough. The subject matter is left for more talented writers, so that what is here hastily set down <note>The catchword "ratum" at the bottom of the page completes the Latin word "deproperatum," meaning "hastily prepared" or "rushed."</note> with a still trembling and tumultuous *style*...

<meta>catchword: settled / completed (part of "hastily set down")</meta>

<summary>Johann Valentin Andreae begins his account of the catastrophic fire in Vaihingen an der Enz, explaining that he is writing as an eyewitness to provide an accurate record against false rumors and to preserve the event as a moral lesson.</summary>

<keywords>Vaihingen an der Enz, Württemberg, Great Fire, eyewitness account, divine judgment, 1618, Enz River, Pforzheim, Bietigheim</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction where Johann Valentin Andreae explains his motivations for writing this account. ->### VAIHINGENSE I.<-

...prepared, they themselves might polish at their leisure and away from danger for the use of either the state or the literary world <note>original: "rei sive publicæ sive literariæ"; a reference to the "Republic of Letters," the international community of scholars.</note>. For the rest, our tragedy unfolds as follows.

On the **first of November**, which is sacred to **All Saints** according to the ancient custom, in the year **1617** after the miraculous birth of the Virgin <note>i.e., the year of our Lord, 1617.</note>, the citizens of **Vaihingen** had prepared themselves for a solemn **Jubilee** established by noble mandate to mark the one hundredth anniversary of the Gospel reborn through **Dr. Luther**. One hundred and forty-five people had presented themselves according to the religious custom of the Church as candidates for **Holy Communion** <note>original: "sacræ Synaxeos"; the author uses the Greek term "synaxis" for the gathering of the faithful for the Eucharist.</note>. Between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, a flame was seen to break out from the house of **Johann Kiefer**, the steward of **Illingen**, which he had granted to his son-in-law **Conrad Machtolf** to live in. It is uncertain by whose fault the fire was lit. Because those buildings were full of grain, they easily gave growth and violence to the fire, bringing terror to those nearby because the house was situated right in the middle of the neighboring buildings. These same houses had burned once before in the year **1607**, but without major damage; and

->## FIRE<-

since that fire, they stood entirely new from their foundations. But now, perhaps through some fatal drowsiness of the citizens, the alarm was raised and the signal of danger given by the bell too late. Meanwhile, from the watchtower—though later than was proper—the neighbors were summoned by the blast of a cannon <note>original: "bombardæ displosione"; cannons were frequently used as emergency signals to alert surrounding villages to fire or invasion.</note>; to which **Asperg** <note>Hohenasperg, a prominent fortress on a hill near Vaihingen, which served as a regional signal point.</note> responded with a great crash, and soon all the bells of the great church rang out with a mournful sound. 

But by now, alas, the flames had grown monstrously powerful and had invaded the neighboring houses on both sides: on one side, the house of **Aurelius Rathgeb**, a judge recently deceased; on the other, that of **Michael Hemminger**, a councilman <term>consular man: a high-ranking member of the city council</term>. These were splendid homes, filled with the bounty of the harvest, which the general opinion suggests could have been saved if there had been less wine and more order, and if communal aid had arrived. 

Furthermore, because in such a great fire everyone wished to look after their own property, and individuals were almost entirely occupied in carrying out their household goods, while the cries and flight of the women and children <note>original: "muliercularum & imbellis ætatis"; literally "little women and those of an age unfit for war," a common Latin idiom for those unable to help in a crisis.</note> caused confusion on all sides, it happened that the disaster seized other nearby places swifter than a word. ...only a few houses; but because at the corner where two streets intersected, the force of the fire reigned supreme, it crossed the streets and flew into the neighboring buildings on every side. It was a truly lamentable sight: with rescue efforts hopeless <note>original: "desperato succursu"</note>, and sometimes with barely a bucket of water poured out, everything was abandoned to the lust of the flames, with only people's lives being saved. 

One could see places that, only a little while before, had served as refuges for the fleeing, sheltering various bundles of belongings as well as cradles and wailing infants; within a few moments, these were invaded by a horrific force, and the trembling crowd was driven from the spot. Good God! What a burdening of mothers? What haste of fathers in pushing their children to safety? What dazed shock in those of such tender age? # THE CONFLAGRATION #

...church dedicated to the Virgin Mother of God <note>original: "Deiparae Virgini" — a Latin title for Mary meaning "the one who gave birth to God," often rendered as "the Mother of God."</note>, a vast and not unhandsome structure, recently made notable by the generosity of the citizens with its organ and sacred paintings. Indeed, such was the bringing in of countless household goods that those who had arrived first, or who had occupied the center, were completely blocked from leaving. But soon terror was brought even to this place, which had been treated like a sacred anchor <note>A metaphor for a final hope or a place of last resort.</note>; for although it rose up to the roof in solid stone, all confidence was nevertheless cut away because of the proximity of the granaries (where the fire had already approached). From this, there followed a chaotic removal of goods by many, and a flight was sought toward the hill where the fortress <term>arx: referring to Schloss Kaltenstein, which towers over Vaihingen</term> sits—whether with greater confusion or trembling, it is uncertain. And indeed, before the eyes of every most prudent man, the entire town now appeared as a funeral pyre. This fear was excited in nearly everyone not so much by the blast of the whirlwind and the vastness of the flame—and the perpetual fuel it found in so many sources—as by the consternation of the citizens and the exhaustion of those rendering aid amidst the extremes of heat and cold. It was then that God, [offering] an illustrious specimen of His power and wisdom, with His accustomed mercy...

# FIRE

...revenues, had caught fire; and already, it had driven away as many as had rushed to help with its intolerable heat. But when several brave men had encouraged themselves once more to save this building, and several smaller houses were being torn down using iron hooks <note>original: "uncis ferreis" — heavy iron hooks used by early firefighters to pull down burning structures or create firebreaks by destroying adjacent wooden buildings.</note> prepared for that purpose, God surely permitted an end to these miseries to emerge here as well.

From that point, the School dedicated to German youth <term>German School: At this time, a "German school" taught basic literacy and numeracy in the vernacular, whereas a "Latin school" prepared students for university.</term> was more easily—yet not without a miracle, I believe—saved. For although it was completely neglected and the fire struck it on both sides persistently, and though men and horses were using great effort to level it to the ground <note>Likely attempting to pull the building down to create a firebreak to stop the flames from spreading further.</note>, it yielded neither to fire nor to iron. Because of this, it drew a few men to itself (among whom **Bernhard Engelhard** must not be left unmentioned), and after a short time, it obtained safety.

For God willed that both training grounds of Christian education <note>original: "pædagogiæ Christianæ utrasque palæstras" — the author uses the Greek term "palæstra" (a wrestling school) as a metaphor for the schools where children were "trained" in faith and knowledge.</note>, and the halls where the Most Holy Word is heard, as well as the dwellings of those who minister in sacred things, should remain standing. Though they were sprinkled on all sides with sparks and there was no one to douse them with water, they remained solid; so that it might be clear that war was being waged not yet against Religion, but against wickedness <note>The sentence ends mid-thought; the catchword indicates the war against wickedness was sent "from the heavens."</note>

The final and most violent conflict was waged from the houses of **Michael Beurlin**, **Bartholomäus Gebelin**, and **Andreas Schmid** against the two houses opposite them, belonging to **Rudolf Haman** and **Johann Wild**, the Surgeon <term>Surgeon: In this era, a "surgeon" (chirurgus) was typically a barber-surgeon who handled wounds, fractures, and surgeries, as distinct from a physician who studied internal medicine.</term>—houses that were, by then, entirely engulfed in flames. Because these houses faced each other in a narrow alley at a crossroads, they compressed the flame as if into a single point, forcing a decisive struggle for the ultimate fate and the safety of the rest of the town.

For by public consensus, the fire had to be stopped here or never; if it were not suppressed at this spot, it would have attacked what was essentially another part of the city. Here, the church faced the greatest danger; here too, the nearest and largest houses; and from there, the town hall <note>original: "curiæ" — the administrative seat of the local government.</note>. What need is there to imagine another such horrible sequence of fire? 

For God was present with those who rushed together, those crying out, those working together, and those gasping for breath. After a long enough delay—caused by the Surgeon's houses, which were both quite new and soaked with burnt wine <note>Wine and spirits were frequently used as bases for medicinal tinctures, making the surgeon's home particularly flammable.</note> and various ointments and oils—at last, ah! at last, it was possible to breathe again and [begin to give thanks...] <meta>catchword: gra- (likely beginning "gratias agere," the Latin phrase for giving thanks).</meta>

...permitted them to lift their hands to the heavens, even amidst the glowing embers and sheer <term>ovens: The Latin "clibanos" refers to earthenware ovens, suggesting the houses had become enclosed chambers of intense heat.</term>. Amidst all this, the fifth hour of the morning was heard striking. Let no one ask with what horror that hour had been expected since the ninth hour of the night <note>9:00 PM</note>, or with what agonizing slowness it finally arrived, unless they wish to move themselves to tears; let no one even try to conceive of it in their mind, except as something that nearly exceeds human endurance. 

Alas! How many houses in the meantime were ruined by their neighbors? The flames, carried along by an unfavorable wind, embraced these buildings and would hardly let them go, and never without significant loss. How many walls were breached? How many roofs smashed? Windows shattered? Doors and gates scorched? Already the rooftops of the houses were burning, then the beams; soon the rooms themselves were set ablaze and glowing with light; then the very centers of the homes were occupied by the fires. 

Each of these disasters on its own could have exhausted a great multitude of men, but in this heap of miseries, such individual losses were regarded with utter neglect and left to the inhabitants of the houses themselves. Through their untiring labor, a long series of houses was saved, most notably those of **Erasmus Kellin**, the great **Hospital** <term>Hospital: In the 17th century, a "hospitale" was typically a charitable institution or almshouse providing care for the elderly and the poor.</term>, **Andreas Schmid**, **Andreas Machtolf**, and **Christopher Mach-** <note>original: "Christoferi Mach-" — the name concludes on the following page.</note>

In total, sixty-one houses and thirty-three barns perished, being reduced entirely to ashes; nine stables were consumed, and eight buildings were wrecked. There are some, however, who count 116 <term>roofs: A traditional method of assessing tax or fire damage by counting individual roof structures rather than legal property deeds.</term> according to the usual manner.

The loss of household goods was not equal among the citizens. For while some were driven out almost naked, many others managed to carry out nearly all their belongings. However, so much wine was lost—as they were less able to carefully seal the cellars <note>original: "obstruere cellas"</note>—that the damage was estimated at eight hundred and forty-six casks or large jars. A no small amount of gold and silver was also either buried under the rubble or stolen.

Indeed, it must be considered a sign of great Divine kindness that no one was seriously injured, much less in danger of their life, and only one or two were lightly wounded. This is all the more remarkable given that the streets were blocked everywhere with burning beams and falling roof tiles <note>original: "tegularum disjectiones"</note> scattered in every direction. # THE FIRE

...had drawn his breath, he began to crawl out with the fire already nearby. Having been found, he was carried outside the city; from there, when he had been brought back home, on the fifth day afterward—whether from illness or from terror—he departed to God <note>original: "ad Deum emigravit," a common religious euphemism for death</note>. A very few women in labor, including one who had given birth only a day before, found safety through flight. One pregnant woman even gave birth to a premature child; yet the infant was alive and initiated into Christ <note>original: "Christo initiatum," meaning the child was baptized immediately after its birth due to the emergency</note>. 

This monstrous fire raged no further against human life, except that everything resounded with wailing and public mourning. For the lamentation that had until now been restrained by the sheer magnitude of the immediate danger now broke out openly, as a vast multitude moved into the homes of others, saying a bitter farewell to their own. 

With a painful recounting of the labors they had once endured for their homes, their small beds, and their household goods, they remembered how much this meager support for life—whatever it had been—had cost them in effort. 

Meanwhile, the long-awaited Sun looked down somewhat sadly upon our pyres <note>The author uses the word "rogos" (pyres) to describe the smoldering ruins of the houses</note>, when the City Magistrate, having called together those who had rushed to assist, kindly gave thanks for their devotion. # VAIHINGENSE I.
