...it was clear they had come <note>Completes the thought from the previous page: "those whom [from afar] it was clear they had come."</note>, he dismissed them. He entreated those who lived closer to extinguish the still-burning hearths; an entire day was so consumed by this task that the reward for their effort and labor hardly seemed apparent. Because of this, watches were established to guard the heaps of glowing embers, and men with carts were sent from neighboring towns and the **Maulbronn Monastery** <note>original: "Maulbronnensi Cœnobio"; a prominent Cistercian monastery nearby</note>, by whose assistance the smoldering logs <note>original: "titiones"</note> might be carried out of the city. 

On that day, several cellars were opened, and no small amount of wine—still boiling from the heat—and a significant number of household goods were rescued. Furthermore, vessels made of various metals were dug out, as well as much coinage that had already been melted into formless lumps. 

However, where the unbearable heat would not permit the diggers to enter, the losses were repeatedly compounded. For, as the iron hoops of the barrels burst from the intense heat, the wine flowed out, sometimes in such volume that the cellars were filled to a depth of two feet; some of this was eventually pumped out. 

Meanwhile, the violence of the winds did not let up; during these days, they blew in a completely unusual and furious manner, [stirring up] the fire lurking beneath the ashes into a dread-

# THE FIRE
->Page 234<-

...ful spectacle <note>Completes the word "formidable" from the previous page: *formidabili*.</note> through the ruins and even the houses that were still intact. From this arose greater anxiety for the watchmen, less rest for the citizens, and no sense of security even in those dwellings that remained unharmed. Indeed, few possessed such boldness that they dared to reclaim their belongings which had been scattered here and there across the crossroads and gardens. This delay provided an opportunity for many men of desperate malice to handle and steal the property of others <note>The author highlights the breakdown of social order and the opportunistic looting that followed the disaster.</note>.

The following day, as well as those that came after, were devoted to clearing the streets and leveling the ruins. At that time, envoys were sent by the **Most Illustrious Prince of the Fatherland, Johann Friedrich** <note>Johann Friedrich (1582–1628) was the Duke of Württemberg; he was known for his efforts to rebuild his territories during the early years of the Thirty Years' War.</note>: the most distinguished **Johann Sattler**, a member of the privy council <note>original: *à secretioribus*</note>, and the most ingenious architect **Heinrich Schickhardt** <note>original: *Henricus Schichardus*; a renowned Renaissance architect and engineer often called the "Swabian Leonardo," who designed many important buildings in Württemberg.</note>. Their task was to witness the face of this tragic scene in person and to provide a report with an accurate drawing <note>original: *accuratâ delineatione*; this suggests a formal architectural or survey map was commissioned to document the damage.</note>.

Indeed, towards evening, the **Most Merciful Prince** himself arrived with his princely brother, **Lord Achilles**, accompanied by a small retinue. ...at midday, after a lengthy declaration of clemency and a promise to provide aid, he departed appearing much like a mourner. A short time later, when the Church—which had been filled with the salvaged bundles and belongings of the people—was unlocked, another scene of chaos unfolded. Like a swarm of ants, or rather hornets, the wandering populace rushed in. Driven either by a desperate longing for their own possessions or by the fear of thieves (who were as numerous as they were bold), they fell upon the nearly infinite variety of household goods with such shouting, quarreling, and noise that it seemed a riot was imminent. The air rang with accusations, denials, and the resulting insults.

To restrain this ugly state of affairs, the Dean of the Church, **Melchior Bengel** <note>Melchior Bengel (1569–1645) served as the Special Superintendent (Dean) in Vaihingen; he was a key figure in organizing the spiritual and practical recovery of the town after the 1618 fire.</note>, climbed into the pulpit and with solemn words exhorted the turbulent crowd toward their Christian duty and civil modesty. However, the crowd hardly quieted down until the obstructions were cleared away—which was done more quickly than it can be described—though it resulted in the loss of property for some. 

Once the sacred building had been cleaned and Wednesday <note>original: *Mercurii dies*; literally "the day of Mercury," the standard Latin term for Wednesday.</note> had dawned, the citizens were called together for an assembly <note>original: *concionem*; a formal gathering for a sermon or public address.</note>. ### THE FIRE

The Minister, having recited the words of Saint David from **Psalm 115, verse 71** <note>This citation follows an older numbering; in modern Bibles, this verse is found at Psalm 119:71.</note>:
> *“It was good for me that I was afflicted, so that I might learn your statutes,”*

I urged them to acknowledge the severity of the Supreme Judge, who, after frequent warnings from His word and hindered by no human obstacles, carries out His burning wrath <note>original: *excandescentiam*; literally "a glowing heat," used here to describe the white-hot anger of God</note>. I exhorted them to disregard earthly riches which, though sought with such danger to the soul and torments to the body, so suddenly and with a grim face abandon those they once enslaved. I called them to embrace golden peace, and to dread the scourges of God that are inflicted upon the discordant and the restless. I asked them to value Divine providence, which preserved such a numerous and confused crowd unharmed in the midst of so great a danger. I told them to look to their neighbor—who might once have been treated unworthily or meanly, but whose necessity and use is so great in a time of affliction. Truly, the time will come when a reborn Vaihingen, as it were, will honor its second authors and founders with grateful praise, and will entrust the memory of its former calamity to posterity.

And so far, we have recounted a most mournful and dire event. It remains for us to adore, with humble and contrite hearts amidst our ashes, God the Lover of Humanity <note>original: *Philanthropum*</note>, whose anger is tempered by mercy, and whose indignation is most salutary <note>meaning: beneficial or restorative for the soul</note>. ### THE VAIHINGEN ACCOUNT: I.

...matters. If this mindset could be persuaded to Christians, some would live in less luxury, others would hunger less, and this earth of ours would comfortably suffice for the use of all. We, I say, who carry about the branded marks <note>original: *stigmata*</note> of God's wrath burnt into us, and who even now are still smoking from the Divine white-hot anger? # VAIHINGENSE I.
<note>This title refers to the first in a series of writings or reports concerning the town of Vaihingen an der Enz, where Andreae served as superintendent.</note>

There was hope that, in that first fire by which Vaihingen—before a full year had even passed—was largely devastated (a truly sorrowful sight), the stubborn spirits set against God, the troublesome disputes, the untimely revelries, and similar plagues for which the place was once notorious under many names, had likewise been consumed. It was hoped that a zeal for piety had returned, along with respect for the laws, the bonds of community, and in every way the integrity of the leaders and the obedience of the subordinates.

But everything fell short of hope, and it has been proven by unfortunate evidence that nothing is more dangerous or more wretched than social breakdown. For while in other places "small things grow through harmony," <note>original: "concordiâ res parvæ crescunt." A famous Latin proverb from the historian Sallust, often used to emphasize that unity creates strength while discord destroys it.</note> here for many years now, with rivalries and internal hatreds slithering among the citizens, great and most magnificent things have begun to fall into ruin. 

Evidently, so that the lust and greed of a few might be satisfied; so that one person after another might step into power; so that the "milking" of public offices <note>original: "munerum mulctra." A biting metaphor suggesting that officials treated their positions like a cow to be milked for personal gain.</note> might be perpetual; and so that trivial offenses might be paid for with bitter punishments, the entire city and every innocent person

<meta>catchword: que (part of "quisque," meaning "everyone")</meta>

### VAIHINGENSE I. 243

**THERE WAS HOPE** that, in that first fire by which Vaihingen was devastated—a most sorrowful spectacle indeed, occurring before a full year had even passed—those spirits stubborn against God, the troublesome lawsuits, the untimely revelries, and similar plagues for which this place was once notorious, had likewise been consumed. There was hope that a zeal for piety <note>original: "pietatis zelum." In this context, piety refers to both religious devotion and the fulfillment of social duties.</note> had returned, as well as an observance of the laws and the bonds of community; and that the integrity of the superiors and the obedience of the subordinates had altogether been restored. 

But everything fell short of that hope, and it has been proven by unfortunate evidence that nothing is both more dangerous and more miserable than discord. <note>original: "dissolutione," referring here to the breaking apart of social and moral order or a "dissolving" of the community spirit.</note> For while in other places "small things grow through harmony," <note>This is a famous Latin proverb from the Roman historian Sallust: *concordia res parvae crescunt*.</note> here for many years now, as rivalries and internal hatreds slither among the citizens, great and magnificent things have begun to fall into ruin. 

Evidently, this is happening so that the lust and greed <term>ingluviei: literally "gluttony," used here to describe the voracious corruption of local officials</term> of a few might be satisfied; so that one person after another may take their turn at power; so that the "milking" <term>mulctra: literally a milking-pail, used here as a metaphor for the constant extraction of wealth or bribes from public offices</term> of public duties might be eternal; and so that trivial offenses might be paid for with bitter punishments, while the entire city and every innocent person...

# FIRE

<header>FIRE</header>

...who was seen to be shamefully manipulated and exploited at the same time. Meanwhile, religious zeal waned, civil order collapsed, and the practice of charity ceased. With the citizens split into rival factions, they reached a point where one side’s only pleasure was to see the other side displeased. In this state of affairs, many believed it was a misery to lead others, to be a subject, or indeed even just to be present there. But the situation turned truly bitter when it became clear that God’s direct correction <note>original: "correptionem," a term implying both a rebuke and a physical punishment meant to reform the soul.</note> was being spurned, and that his repeated lashes failed to move these hardened hearts <note>original: "durissimos humeros," literally "the hardest shoulders," a metaphor for stubbornness or a refusal to bow under divine discipline.</note> in the slightest. 

By now, Vaihingen had risen, so to speak, from its ashes. Out of roughly one hundred and twenty ruins, about fifty houses had been rebuilt. These were impressive in their design and construction—though one or two were perhaps more ostentatious and likely to provoke envy than was appropriate for the circumstances. The charitable generosity of the surrounding country <note>original: "communis patriæ," referring to the broader region or Duchy of Württemberg.</note> had contributed nearly twelve thousand florins <term>florenus: a florin or guilder, the standard currency of the time; 12,000 was a significant sum for disaster relief</term> for this purpose. However, by the typical calculations of the world, this aid was distributed more lavishly to the wealthy than to the truly needy. To put it briefly: there were some for whom this tragedy was actually profitable—even though the total loss to the citizens <note>The text ends mid-word with "clades ci-" and the catchword "vium," completing "civium."</note> exceeded eighty thousand...

<header>VAIHINGENSE II.</header>

He was followed by Johannes Wild, a surgeon <term>chirurgus: in this era, a "barber-surgeon" who handled practical medical tasks like setting bones or surgery</term>, who was the very first to move into his own house—the same house we previously mentioned had been destroyed by the fire. 

From that point, buildings emerged one after another, but the most impressive was the construction by our Most Illustrious Prince <note>The Duke of Württemberg, who oversaw the rebuilding of the town’s prominent administrative buildings</note>. He restored the ancient seat of the former Counts of Vaihingen, though he repurposed the site for different uses. 

Meanwhile, due to the arrogance of the laborers and the indulgence of the citizens, the town was filled with the noise of dancing, shouting, and drinking parties—even during forbidden hours. This led to frequent quarrels while the Magistrates either gave way to the chaos or simply turned a blind eye. Indeed, although the fire had swallowed up eight hundred and forty-six large casks <term>amphora: a large liquid measure; the loss of 846 casks highlights the massive destruction of the town's wine stores</term>, there was certainly no less drinking afterward, and no one was any quicker to rise from their cups. 

The new houses were "consecrated" with heavy drunkenness. You would have seen even children acting like wild revelers <note>original: "bacchari," to behave like followers of Bacchus, the god of wine</note>. The clergy cried out against these things in vain and were met with resentment. While the ministers warn of bad omens, remind individuals of their duties, and echo the complaints of upright citizens, they are listened to with malice. Those who, having moved into the houses of others with the expected...

# THE FIRE

Those who had been welcomed out of compassion were now ruling over the inhabitants with violence and pride. They broke into homes and smashed property, adding threats of terror to ensure that no one would dare mutter a word of protest. For anyone who dared to lament the injuries they suffered, or who spoke out against those secret and most unjust legal maneuvers, was immediately accused of treason <term>perduellio: a grave charge of high treason or hostility against the state</term>. 

Because of this wretched state of affairs—with the arrogant on one side and the downtrodden on the other—a stern and just God visited the people of Vaihingen once again on Friday, October 9th, between two and three o'clock in the afternoon <note>original: "horâ 2. & 3. pomeridianâ"</note>. The fire began in the house of Jacob Semiller, a farmer; it is said that the cause was a live ember, still glowing beneath the ashes, which had been carelessly thrown away. 

Once the fire took hold, it easily consumed the ancient house, breaking through both the upper and lower levels. However, it became most lethal when it dropped onto the straw stored below. Thus, with the house on one side and the barn on the other both ablaze, the citizens were alerted to a danger of the highest order. The speed of the flames was so great that the mother of the household barely managed to save herself by leaping through the windows after throwing her children out to safety. Even so, her nine-year-old daughter, Barbara, was so badly burned by the flames that two days later...

### VAIHINGEN

...she did not escape death. <note>Referring to Barbara, the child mentioned on the previous page who died two days after the fire began.</note> From there, as the calamity <note>original: "malo," meaning the fire or misfortune</note> invaded not only the neighbors on both sides, Caspar Eckerstein and Philipp Steinberg, but as the flames from the barn pierced through the adjoining walls of other barns, it easily became apparent that the situation was once again in a most desperate state.

Indeed, to the right, through the vigor of Jacob Bissinger, a Judge <term>Judicis: a local magistrate or member of the town's judicial council</term>, the onslaught of the fire was contained within Caspar's house. No less effort was made at the front, which faced the market square <term>forum: the central public square of the town</term>; there, the Town Hall <term>Curia: the building housing the city administration and council</term>, though separated only by a narrow alley, was saved through immense exertion.

But to the left, there was the greatest misfortune. For here, the fire seized the house of Sebastian Sinskind, an apothecary <term>Pharmacopolæ: a pharmacist or druggist</term>, who had already been severely afflicted by the previous fire. Although his property was bounded by an alley, the flames nevertheless caught both the home of Christofer Hecht—a legal consultant and former Assessor of the Illustrious Court <term>Dicasterij: a high judicial body or state department</term>—and, across the alley, the house of the widow of Johann Kilian Herger. Each of these buildings brought ruin and destruction to its own neighborhood.

From Hecht’s property, the fire spread to that of Johann Ulrich Bengel, who oversees the Prince’s Fisheries <note>An official responsible for managing the fishing rights belonging to the Duke of Württemberg</note>; from Herger’s property, in a longer chain of destruction, it reached the house of Agatha, a woman of decrepit old age, and that of Matthias Hau—

# THE FIRE

...[of Johann Geor]gius <note>This fragment completes the name of a resident from the previous page.</note>, Dr. Johann Georg Hamberger, the City Physician <term>Physici: the official medical officer appointed by the town council</term>; and the home of Anna, who was the mother of the late Michael Wisefaut, a man who earned the highest merit in the field of scholarship <note>original: "de re literariâ," referring to his contributions to the 'Republic of Letters' or the academic community</note>. The fire completely leveled these dwellings, at which spot, by God's mercy, the flames finally subsided.

The rear of the first barn was also extremely destructive. It devastated three barns that were interspersed among the houses; this presented a greater danger of the fire spreading than the actual value of the property lost. For aside from the very spacious houses of Conrad Schropp and Johann Adam Hamm, the remaining structures were built more for utility than for architectural beauty. Nevertheless, the significant loss of household goods <term>Supellectilium: furniture, clothing, tools, and other domestic property</term> increased the local grief, as such losses are always hardest on those who are already in need.

I return now to the joined houses of Hecht and Bengel. These homes had already consumed very valuable household goods, but when the disaster spread through the adjoining barn and the neighboring house of Andreas Lauterhar—who served for many years as the church sacristan <term>æditui: the official responsible for the maintenance of the church building and its contents</term>—it brought irreparable damage to the city. For nearby stood the Great Church <note>original: "Templum," referring here to the town's main parish church</note>, a work of most majestic construction, which by the devotion of our ancestors was dedicated to the Virgin Mother of God. # VAIHINGENSE II.

[the tower, which was] not built of solid stone, but rather topped with a wooden spire <note>original: "pyramide fastigiata," referring to the steeple's pyramidal shape</note>, caught fire and cast it into the rest of the building. To prevent this disaster from being countered, the extremely faulty stairs—which provided only a very narrow and difficult ascent—hindered efforts greatly. But what was most damaging was that the upper timber-work <term>contabulationi: the wooden flooring or framework of the ceiling and roof</term> of the church could only be reached by crawling through a single, very narrow opening. Because the fire had already taken hold of this spot, it simultaneously cut off all human aid. It taught a lesson—alas, too late!—that the warning about this architectural error had not been raised in vain, nor had the urging that the *City Wardens* <term>Ædilibus: officials responsible for the maintenance of public buildings</term> correct it been without cause. 

And so, once the fire was admitted into the inner sanctuary <note>original: "penetralia"</note> of the church and began to rage, witnesses say that everything resounded in a horrific manner with a strange crashing and the roar of a driving wind. For whatever remained of the benches and pews was joined by the falling roof, turning the interior into something like a burning furnace. # THE FIRE

It is pleasing here to sketch the appearance of our venerable temple with a simple compass <note>original: "rudi circino," a metaphorical way of saying a basic or rough architectural outline</note>. It was a rectangular building, 120 feet long, 78 feet wide, and 70 feet high at the central area where it rose the highest. For with its wings <note>original: "alis," referring to the side aisles</note> added on each side, it reached a height of only 36 feet. There had been added to each side six vaulted chambers <note>original: "concamera-tæ cellulæ," likely referring to side chapels or niches</note> of slightly lesser height.

The entire structure was divided into nearly twelve equal parts by ten very robust columns; these the fire has now almost entirely consumed. For even though the entire massive building had been constructed of cut and squared stone, it nevertheless could not withstand the cruelty of Vulcan <note>the Roman god of fire, used here as a personification of the blaze</note>. This very spacious temple succeeded a smaller chapel on the second of August in the year 1513. It was built through various funds, notably the memorials of the Gremp family <note>The Gremp von Freudenstein family, a prominent local noble lineage and patrons of the church</note>, who are descended from a most ancient lineage among us. Chief among these was *Henry*, who died piously in December 1537 and was honored for it with both a Latin and German epitaph; *Dionysius* followed his example in 1534

These works, having been pursued and for the most part completed through the laudable readiness of the citizens, rendered the church most beautiful, and comparable to very few others in our region. <header>VAIHINGEN II.</header>

...efforts, and [the fire] disdained the **melody of the Organ** <note>original: "Organi melos"</note>, and whatever echoed Christ with an external sound; and it cast away our colors, and whatever represented **Christ** <note>original: "Christus"</note> in an external painting. For while that music sounded with Divine praises, it did so with a clearly profane disposition, and a most immediate corruption of the youth. It also held paintings of Christ at his birth, receiving gifts from the **Magi** <note>original: "Magis"</note>, ascending into Heaven, and returning once again; it featured, moreover, **David’s Lyre** <note>original: "Davidis Cytharam"</note> and the harmonies of his singers, but with a spirit less inclined toward Christ.

Whatever we now praise, we now see has displeased God; for we believed in our hearts that God could be captured—or more truly, deceived—by the painting of stones and wood while our souls were deformed; by the cultivation of the temple while our souls were filthy; and by windy pomp and the **luxury of the paintbrush** <note>original: "penicilli luxurie"</note> while we were lacking in piety.

Indeed, the **ornaments of His Sanctuary** <note>original: "Sanctuarij ornamenta"</note> are entirely pleasing to our God; for this reason, I do not regret having encouraged my friends, nor will I allow those who contributed their own wealth here to depart unpraised, even in the midst of our mourning. [...was] given with a sincere mind <note>original: "candidâ mente"</note>, God has called to Himself and has inscribed in an everlasting memorial. Let us learn from this, however, to empty ourselves for God, to cleanse ourselves, and to introduce the ornaments of a truer Christianity; having been refined by these, we will easily despise all earthly brilliance, pretense, and superficial coatings.

But to return to our tragedy, a triple evil threatened the town from the lamented fire of the one church. For in the part that faces the East, where the <term>Adytum</term> <note>the innermost sanctuary of a church; the original Latin term refers to a space not to be entered by the uninitiated</note> or—as they call it—the **Choir**, remained unfinished (due, I believe, to a lack of funds or the disturbances of the **Reformation** <note>original: "mutatæ Religionis," literally "the changed religion"</note>), the buildings of the illustrious **Teutonic Order** <note>original: "Ordinis Teutonici," a medieval Catholic military order that held significant land and influence</note>, located only a few feet away, began to suffer.

This most serious danger was overcome by the aforementioned **Johann Jakob Engelhard**, the Order's **Steward** <note>original: "Oeconomus"</note>, to his own benefit as well as that of the entire neighborhood. On the other, northern side, the **Latin School** <note>original: "Pædagogium Latinum"</note> struggled against the nearby and rushing flames, with the vigorous fighter **Master Wilhelm Cuppelius**, the head of the literary youth <note>referring to his role as schoolmaster</note>, leading the defense.

->## VAIHINGEN<-

The third struggle, but by far the most severe, occurred at the **Parsonage** <note>original: "Parochialibus ædibus"</note>, which was my home at that time. These buildings were endangered not so much by the threat of the church itself, but by the burning house of my neighbor **Johann Göppinger**, a member of the city council <note>original: "viri Senatorij"</note>; the wind, blowing against us, worsened the situation.

I was away at the time, visiting friends, and specifically devoting a day or two to my most esteemed friend **Master Wilhelm Schickard** <note>Wilhelm Schickard (1592–1635) was a famous polymath and professor at Tübingen, credited with inventing the first mechanical calculator</note>. My wife, who had recently given birth <term>puerpera</term> <note>a woman in the period of recovery immediately following childbirth, often considered a time of great physical vulnerability</note> and was further afflicted by other oncoming illnesses, saw the fire before she even heard it was coming. She had barely wrapped herself in her nightgown when it was time to flee. Nevertheless, she managed to pack up a few small belongings <note>original: "reculas aliquas"</note> and snatched them from the most imminent danger. 

She shared this brave lot with four other women in childbed, who had been allowed only a very few days to recover after giving birth. Once again, sanctuary was found in the illustrious castle, provided by that most kind hostess **Lady Elizabeth von Laimingen**, the wife of **Francis Ludwig Zorn of Bulach**, the High Governor <note>original: "Præfectus Major," the district's chief administrative official</note>. To that same place was brought the wife of the Dean, **Melchior Bengel**, who for many weeks already—

...confined very heavily to her bed. <note>The Latin text begins with the end of a word, "nis," likely completing a description of the author's wife being carried on her bed-frame or pallet.</note> Thus, just as her husband **von Bulach** vigorously spent himself until the middle of the night and beyond in advising, directing, and consulting; so she herself, by receiving, guarding, and tending to the refugees, fulfilled every measure of the most noble piety, each doing their part.

Meanwhile, my own belongings were not entirely abandoned. Through the faithfulness of several friends—chiefly **Master Caspar Dirrius**, a colleague from our **Pedagogium** <note>The Pedagogium was a state-run secondary school intended to prepare students for higher university studies</note>; and later **Master Wolfgang Mögling**, the Pastor of **Lammersheim**; as well as **Martin Rotacker**, the joiner—my goods were loosened, thrown out, and carried or dragged into the open as much as could be done in that tumultuous haste. I am amazed that these things, however much they were ultimately diminished, returned to me in any significant number, given such a multitude of thieves—or rather, sacrilegious looters. 

For no one will easily believe me as I write that such is the cruelty of some "Christians" that they would rob and plunder their own neighbors in their most afflicted state, even while the wrath of God was seething <note>original: "æstuante Dei irâ"</note> around them. **Dirrius** afterwards performed a praiseworthy service, when...

<meta>catchword: Orga-</meta>

VAIHINGENSE II. 261

...[He] pulled the pipes of the **Musical Organ** <note>original: "Organi Musici"</note> from the fire. Others did the same in dismantling the **Clock** <note>The tower clock was a vital piece of civic technology in the 17th century</note>, a feat as praiseworthy as it was beneficial. Whatever had to be saved could not suffer delay. For within three hours, in addition to the vast **church** <note>The Stadtkirche (City Church) of Vaihingen</note>, twenty-three houses and five barns collapsed. This was a smaller number of buildings lost and a shorter duration than the previous fire <note>The author likely refers to a fire in 1591 or the initial destruction earlier in the Thirty Years' War</note>, but in the judgment of every prudent person, the damage was greater and the disaster more intense. 

For those who were present at the scene believe that if the disaster had broken out at night, the immediate death of many people would have been certain—death which they barely escaped even by day and while fully clothed. Furthermore, they consider the poverty of the victims, which allows no hope of recovery <note>original: "respirationis," literally "breathing-room"</note> at all. Finally, above all else, they rightly value the structure of the immense church, whose five bells alone—not so much melted as consumed by the heat of the fire—cannot be replaced even for many thousands of **florins** <note>A gold or silver coin used as a standard currency across Europe</note>. An end was indeed brought to the fire in this part...

-># THE FIRE<-

[The stop was] made in my own and the neighboring houses, which the fire had so besieged that water could not be introduced unless the walls were bored through from the opposite side. Elsewhere, the house of **Franciscus Ferber** <note>A local citizen or perhaps a minor official whose property was sacrificed to save others</note> was leveled to the ground with **iron hooks** <note><term>iron hooks</term>: Long-handled tools used by early modern firefighters to pull down burning timbers or entire buildings to create a firebreak.</note>; this ended the misery, though not without long-lasting toil. Likewise, the public buildings where the **City Scribe** <note>original: "urbis scriba." The town’s head clerk or notary, a position of significant administrative importance</note> lives were saved by the immense efforts of those nearby. 

For it was at this spot that the evil had to be restrained, or else it would have been unleashed upon a great portion of the city. Therefore, they threw themselves into the work with even greater spirit to overcome the **savage enemy** <note>The author personifies the fire as a "savage enemy" (sævum hostem), a common metaphor in early modern disaster accounts</note>. They say that nearly the entire town was emptied of goods and furniture, as the people were terrified by the previous loss <note>A reference to a previous fire or the general trauma of the Thirty Years' War</note>. However, this was done unwisely, for while the citizens were occupied elsewhere and scattered <note>The author suggests that by focusing on saving their furniture, the citizens neglected the organized firefighting effort</note>, the force of the fire grew stronger more quickly and over a wider area. 

But this, like our other misfortunes, we owe to that **dissolution** <note>original: "dissolutioni." A term implying a breakdown of social order, discipline, or civic duty</note> which I condemned at the start, through which we have unlearned both how to command and how to obey. Nor does it appear in prosperous times how dangerously and precariously <note>original: "lubricè," literally "slippery"</note> we live, subject to inexperienced men, until some heavy accident [occurs]...

->## VAIHINGEN II.<-
<meta>catchword: rus.</meta>

[...gra]ver situation calls for men. Indeed, to scold, to threaten, to dominate, and even to drink healths is quite easy; however, to rule with prudence and strength is not equally straightforward. The virtue of a single man builds up much; the vice of a single man destroys even more. For while nothing is lacking for Vaihingen to reach its bloom and the best state of the Commonwealth <note>original: "Reipub." (Respublica), referring here to the civic body or "commonwealth" of the town</note>, a single **Erinnys** <note>In Greek mythology, an Erinnys is a Fury. Here, the author uses it as a personification of "Discord" or "Strife"</note> has weakened even the best men in their best pursuits. 

This spirit of strife has made the **Ministry of the Holy Word** <note>The local Lutheran clergy</note> despondent, since it neither dares nor is able to do anything against the **wealthier citizens** <note>original: "pinguiores," literally "the fatter ones," referring to the town's elite or the "fat cats" of the era</note>. This has made the leading men dissolute, as they devote themselves more to their bellies than to the Commonwealth. This has made the citizens arrogant, since they easily cast off authority under the protection of anyone they choose. This has made individuals distrustful, since they see business being handled by secret plots rather than by manly and open disagreement. There is a golden saying, and deservedly to be repeated by us:

> Through harmony small things grow; through discord great things fall apart. <note>A famous quote from the Roman historian Sallust in his work "The War with Jugurtha"</note>

As night drew near, there was need for many watches and also much labor, lest the <gloss>recurring</gloss> evil <note>The Latin text has a small "r" glossed over "evil," likely shorthand for "rursus" (again) or "recens" (recent), referring to the fire breaking out again</note> should revive; these efforts continued even until daybreak. While during this time, being ignorant of all things—

I hasten home to the countryside. In Stuttgart, indeed, that most distinguished man, **Dr. Erasmus Grüninger** <note>Erasmus Grüninger (1566–1644) was a prominent Lutheran theologian and the Superintendent of the church in Stuttgart.</note>, the reverend provost of the Church, concealed the matter, as it had not yet been fully explained; but a little way from the gate, my most talented friend **Johann Grezmejer** <note>Johann Georg Grezmejer was a court architect for the Duchy of Württemberg, active in rebuilding efforts during the Thirty Years' War.</note>, the Prince's Architect, acting as an eyewitness, revealed the misfortune. From there, I flew toward my own ashes and hearths, and I found my house indeed scorched and torn apart, and my family and small belongings scattered. Many crowds of weeping citizens surrounded the area, either seeking solace or sharing their grief. The fire spared my books, if I still have any; but the **unfaithful hand** <note>A likely reference to looters or thieves who took advantage of the chaos during the fire.</note> did not. It was savage toward others: for both the most well-stocked and elegant library of **Dr. Hecht**, and the very noble books of **Dr. Hamberger**, and the **literary apparatus** <note>original: "literaria supellex," referring to the collective books, notes, and scholarly tools of a professional.</note> of **Balinger**, formerly the preacher of Weissach, were consumed, besides other smaller collections not to be looked down upon. As for me, as soon as I looked upon my remains, I learned that I had possessed too much. Certainly, we live more freely with less; not that the soul...

Whoever possesses **Christ** easily does without all things; whoever gives themselves to **Christ**, along with Him, easily obtains all things worthy of our nobility. ### THE FIRE

The story of the **paralytic** <note>original: "Paralyticum"; referring to the healing of the paralyzed man in Matthew 9. The author is likely referring to the sermon he or the Dean delivered.</note> I adapted to our own ailments as much as I was able; it was received thereafter with a harder demeanor and heard with drier eyes. For we are accustomed to lament our own misfortunes while ignoring God's case against us, and we bear our lot most grudgingly. The ruins of the **Church** <note>original: "Templi"; referring to the Stadtkirche of Vaihingen destroyed in the fire.</note>, lamentable as they truly are, struck everyone with awe; yet the fragments of the heart and the ruins of so many souls were held of very little account. 

Certainly, the **Dean of the Church** <note>original: "Ecclesiæ Decanus"</note>—whom I mention with nothing but honor—took great pains during this interval between the fires to remind the citizens, even those lost in the grief of their material loss, of the spiritual lesson to be learned. He did this by adapting a passage from the **Prophet Amos**, chapter 4, verses 4, 5, and 6 <note>In this biblical passage, the prophet recounts how God sent various disasters, including fire, yet the people "have not returned to Me."</note>, explaining it through several sermons. 

But he himself complained that he made little progress, and others even boasted of this. What, then, shall we say? The world is incorrigible; for whichever way you turn it, it is as prickly as a **hedgehog** <note>original: "Echini"; can refer to a hedgehog or a sea urchin. The metaphor implies the world is impossible to handle or reform without being stung.</note>. Truly, if I may speak candidly, the fault of a few involves the many; and the crime of some becomes the infamy of all. For while **Vaihingen** possesses very many excellent citizens, there are some of the worst sort [who...]

# VAIHINGENSE II.

...they strip away all reputation, order, and religion, and from among these they select such things as it is either right, or useful, or necessary to follow. Consequently, there is not so much a gossip from others about our confusion, as there is our own very frequent outcry <note>original: "quiritatio"; a formal or public wailing/protest</note>; just as we torment ourselves, so we bewail ourselves. We consult Physicians in vain, for we see that they do not desire our well-being, but our money.

The following days, which were also busy with the grape harvest, were dedicated to cooling the ruins. From the tower, they gathered the bronze <note>original: "æs"; specifically the bell metal</note> that remained of the bells, though it was of the very smallest weight <note>The intense heat of the fire would have melted the massive church bells into puddles of metal, leaving only a fraction of their original mass salvageable.</note>. 

On the 20th of October, indeed, the most illustrious Prince of the Country, **Johann Friedrich** <note>Johann Friedrich (1582–1628), the Duke of Württemberg.</note>, arrived with his most distinguished brothers, **Julius Friedrich** <note>Julius Friedrich of Württemberg-Weiltingen (1588–1635).</note> and **Magnus** <note>Magnus of Württemberg-Neuenbürg (1594–1622).</note>, along with that most noble hero, **Kraft, Count of Hohenlohe** <note>original: "Cratone, Hohenloio Comite"; Kraft VII, Count of Hohenlohe-Neuenstein (1582–1641).</note>. He saw the ruins of the church—he saw them, I say, and groaned—and he conceived a noble and generous intention to restore them. He initiated this work through **Heinrich Schickhardt** <note>Heinrich Schickhardt (1558–1635) was the preeminent Renaissance architect and engineer of the Duchy of Württemberg.</note>, his renowned Architect. From here, on the 26th of October, the site <note>catchword: "purga-"; likely "purgari," meaning the site began to be cleared or purged of debris.</note>

->THE FIRE<-

Once the site had been cleared and the stones removed through public labor, it appeared that the columns and arches were the most severely damaged. Furthermore, the tower, where it ends at the gallery <note>original: "deambulacrum"; likely a walkway or belfry level</note>, gapes open, cracked and ruined. And this was indeed the second plague of Vaihingen, occurring when that over-celebrated **DIonYsIUs DeManDeD the pUnIshMent of the CIty** <note>This sentence contains a chronogram: **DIonIsIVs pœnaM CIVItatIs repetIIt**. In the original Latin, the Roman numerals (D+I+I+V+I+V + M + C+I+V+I+I+I) add up to 1617, the year of the fire. "Dionysius" refers to St. Dionysius’ Day, October 9th, when the fire occurred. The author may also be subtly blaming the "cult of Dionysus" (drunkenness) for the tragedy.</note>. Both plagues were certainly full of Divine wrath, and the damage is so harmful that it cannot be repaired in just a few years. For both private and public affairs have been cut down as much as possible; and would that we might obtain this, even at the greatest expense: that hereafter we may be ruled prudently, serve generously, and live appropriately! 

As for the stories that have been spread about us among others, there is no need to refute them here; it is the habit of men to gossip and be easily persuaded, to deceive and be deceived. Furthermore, what shall become of us, the most merciful God will see—He who cannot fail His own, nor wishes to support the impious. The poverty of the afflicted sobs; greed rebukes the wealthy, warning them not to help the needy, and for this, lies are employed. I, for my part, earnestly [commend] the most wretched lot of these poor souls to every good Christian...

-># VAIHINGENSE II.<-

...I commend and promise as a **pledge** <term>original: "vadimonium"; a legal term for a guarantee or bail, here used as a spiritual assurance</term> before God. Whatever you may object against us, remember that a sheep never divides the spoils with a lion on equal terms <note>This is an allusion to Aesop's fables, suggesting that the poor and the powerful are not on equal footing when it comes to resources</note>. Though some unworthy people may have received your donation, many more who were most worthy received it with tears and prayers for your sake. 

But if you claim everyone is unworthy, I beseech you, do not make your **alms** <term>original: "eleemosynas"; charitable gifts given as a religious duty</term> "leaden" <note>To give "leaden" alms is to give reluctantly or in a way that feels heavy and burdensome rather than cheerful</note>; instead, let your luxury in other matters be as light as a feather. If you reproach God for a single small gift, He will reproach you for a hundredfold of your own follies. We are such that, because we perform any good deed only with great difficulty, we always boast of it, while our habitual evils simply flow past us unnoticed. 

From the **pulpit** <term>original: "suggestu"; the raised platform in the church from which the pastor addresses the congregation</term>, we have given solemn thanks to God, to the most illustrious **Prince** <term>original: "Princeps"; referring to Duke Johann Friedrich of Württemberg</term>, and to every good man; and we led the way in praying for several thousand blessings. Luxury and insolence have never pleased us, but there is no one to restrain them. Where whatever is pleasing is permitted, that which is not permitted easily becomes pleasing <note>original: "Ubi licet, quod libet, facilè libet, quod non licet." This is a clever Latin wordplay on the similarity between "licet" (it is permitted) and "libet" (it is pleasing)</note>. May God rule us, so that we may be content with the very least for ourselves and **generously** divide what we have with others. The former will make us more secure from the world; the latter less...

# FIRE

...**to be envied**. Certainly, even to drag the body around is a heavy burden and contrary to true **wealth** <term>original: "opulentiæ"; referring here to spiritual abundance rather than material riches</term>; but God Himself has imposed this burden, and with it certain necessities that no one can change. We, however, how gladly we impose weight upon weight until we stuff our houses—houses that are thereafter exposed to a thousand thousand dangers and inconveniences. Even when we have saved everything and passed it on to our distant descendants, the **final fire** <note>A reference to the biblical "last fire" or "conflagration" believed to consume the world at the end of time</note> will devour all. 

Let this be God's school: to be taught not only how to live without many things, but to patiently return even our necessities to God. Yet we should realize that it is among God’s punishments and lashes when He must snatch away earthly goods from those who are unwilling to let go. In His anger, He takes away what He once graciously gave. He is clearly wonderful in how He treats His own people, whom He blesses through **poverty** <term>original: "indigentia"; the state of lacking material goods, framed here as a spiritual benefit</term>, withdrawing the earth from them so that He might make them capable of receiving heaven. 

Let the first things yield so that the highest may follow, and let this place be emptied by **elementary fire** <note>The physical fire that destroyed the town's buildings</note> so that it may be ready to receive the **super-elementary** fire <note>The spiritual or divine fire that purifies the soul</note>. Why do we mourn the stone temple, and not rather change the **stony heart** <note>A reference to Ezekiel 36:26, "I will remove from you your heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh"</note> into a fleshy **sanctuary** <term>original: "adytum"; the innermost, most sacred part of a temple where the deity resides</term> of God? Chan—

He urges the reader to focus on the transformation of the inner heart rather than the loss of physical buildings.</summary>

### VAIHINGENSE II.
->H 2<-

...do we implore? If God is with us, a seat will not be lacking for Him; nor will there be lacking the means by which we might restore, consecrate, and adorn a house for Him. Why do we seek an **altar** <term>original: "aram"; the physical place of sacrifice and worship in the ruins</term>, when we have approached as **unwashed guests**? <note>A reference to the biblical requirement of spiritual cleanliness before approaching God, particularly the parable of the wedding feast.</note> Why do we clear the sites of fallen houses, while we fill the **building of the spirit** with various defilements through the exile of the **sacred Ruler** <term>original: "sacri Rectoris"; referring to the Holy Spirit or God's sovereignty over the soul</term>? 

Why do we desire household goods, when our own members are unfit and ungrateful to God—indeed, even resistant instruments? Why do we seek other clothes, when we lack that **sacred wedding garment**? <note>Referring to Matthew 22:11-13, where a guest is cast out for not wearing the proper spiritual attire of faith and repentance.</note> Why do we seek out coins, when we disdain that **true sufficiency**? <note>Spiritual contentment or the grace of God, which is "enough."</note> Why do we look for our wines again, when the **ambrosia of heaven** <term>original: "cœli ambrosia"; a classical metaphor for divine food or spiritual delight</term> does not taste good to us? 

It is a very small thing for God to enrich us; it is a very great thing to convert us. It is the easiest thing to raise us from the ashes; it is the most difficult to cast down that **insolence of Adam** <note>The inherited pride and rebellion of original sin.</note>. We are twisted, and God never fully corrects us while we are on this earth, since when He offers bread, we prefer stones; when He offers fish, we prefer scorpions. <note>A rhetorical reversal of Matthew 7:9-10; the author suggests humans perversely choose harmful things even when God offers life-giving ones.</note> Of myself I profess this: to have loved certainly...

<summary>The author argues that the physical reconstruction of Vaihingen is secondary to spiritual renewal, rebuking the citizens for mourning their material losses while their souls remain in ruins. He emphasizes that material restoration is easy for God, but the true challenge lies in human repentance and overcoming the pride of "Adam."</summary>

<page-num>272</page-num>
# INCENDIUM
->H 2<-

...the beauty of the **House of God** <term>original: "Domus Dei"</term>, and that he had obtained it through much labor and the usual grace; now to mourn its desolation, and to vow and cherish from the heart its former brilliance; but that he always preferred the **living tabernacles of God** <note>A reference to the bodies and souls of the faithful, echoing the biblical idea that the true temple of God is the human heart.</note>, and that he had especially striven to **whitewash** <note>original: "dealbarem"; likely referring to the Reformation practice of covering over colorful, "idolatrous" Catholic frescoes with plain white lime-wash to simplify the worship space.</note> as many things as possible which were once painted with idols, and to purge them from profane sacrifices. 

For if it were sufficient to worship God with the most elegant structures, no one would be more religious than the **Antichrist**. <note>The author warns that grand architecture can be a mask for spiritual corruption, a common theme in Protestant critiques of the papacy and worldly religious splendor.</note> However, I do not want affected, shabby filth, nor the **unwashed liberality** <term>original: "illutam... liberalitatem"</term> of men toward sacred things. He spoke very pleasing and agreeable things, which made men immune from all expenses toward Christ. 

I would not dare, however, to recite the **wickednesses** <term>original: "nequitias"</term> I have seen in things meant to be given to Christ. The most wealthy, and the most extravagant, enrich Christ with a small coin of wicked money. <note>original: "improbæ monetæ obulo"; an obol was a coin of very little value. The author is scolding the rich for offering their lowest-quality currency as tithes.</note> He has no need of your coins; rather, you are unworthy to give; but yet, why do you not blush within yourself, if you have any **shame**? <term>original: "frontem habes"; literally "to have a forehead," a Latin idiom for the capacity to feel shame or modesty.</term> We certainly labor under an inexcusable desolation, which they introduced...

<summary>The author argues that while physical churches are important, the "living tabernacles" of the human soul are God's preferred dwelling. He harshly rebukes the hypocrisy of wealthy citizens who offer only their most worthless coins to God while the community suffers in the wake of the fire.</summary>

<keywords>living tabernacles, spiritual architecture, iconoclasm, hypocrisy, church tithes, Antichrist, repentance, Great Fire of Vaihingen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of hypocrisy within the religious community of Vaihingen. He transitions from criticizing the cheapness of the wealthy to a broader social critique, arguing that true faith must be reflected in civil conduct and that the law is necessary to restrain human vice when spiritual devotion fails.</meta>

<page-num>273</page-num>
# VAIHINGEN II
<note>This title refers to the second part of a series of reflections or reports regarding the city of Vaihingen, likely following the Great Fire of 1617.</note>

->H 3<-

...whenever they have placed **Governance** <term>original: "Politiæ"; here meaning civil administration or the secular state</term> before Religion, the body before the soul, and laws before conscience; we stain the most **Sincere Confession** <note>Likely a reference to the Augsburg Confession, the primary confession of faith for the Lutheran Church, which the author feels is being betrayed by poor behavior.</note> with the most disorderly habits, and while we are superior in our religion, we are the lowest of all in our way of life. Regarding what God thinks of this, He declares it more clearly Himself than we need to investigate: and it is to be feared that where there is the greatest boasting of faith, Christ will find the least faith on that Day of His. 

Nor should we wait for each person to bring themselves into line with the best standard: our earth—or rather our **flesh**—does not possess such generosity and nobility. We must be restrained by laws in a servile manner, so that the greatest, the prominent, the equals, the lesser, and the least do not sin with impunity and with no one speaking out. Here, whoever adapts **divine statutes** <note>The moral laws of God as found in Scripture.</note> to life deserves the highest praise, unlike the **Antichrist**, who devised earthly and carnal things. Indeed, the **Gospel** <term>original: "Evangelium"</term> does not abolish **Governance**, but brings its own with it, and that of the best kind: now, if only Governance would not abolish the Gospel! We define **Christianity** as that which...

<meta>catchword: fer- (likely beginning the word "fert" or "ferre", meaning "carries" or "to bear")</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the hypocrisy of his contemporaries who prioritize secular laws over conscience and warns that a high religious "Confession" is worthless without moral conduct. He argues that civil government and the Gospel should support one another rather than the state suppressing the church.</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, Governance, Gospel vs State, moral reform, Lutheran Confession, divine statutes, social order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of moral hypocrisy. On the previous page, he argued that while the Gospel does not abolish civil governance, it requires its own spirit to be truly fulfilled. He continues here by examining the double standards applied to divine and human laws, using the imagery of the fire that destroyed Vaihingen as a metaphor for divine judgment.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>

# THE FIRE
<note>original: "INCENDIUM"; this refers to the catastrophic fire of 1617 in Vaihingen an der Enz.</note>

...so that we may be able to **bear** it, and he may be able to bear us: but I fear there is another standard with Christ, which excludes most people. This alone betrays us: that **Blasphemy** <term>original: "Blasphemia"; speech or actions concerning God that are considered sacrilegious</term> against God is valued at the least, while injury to a mere man <note>original: "homuncionem"; a diminutive, literally "little man," often used to emphasize human insignificance compared to the divine.</note> is valued at the most. Who would believe that through **Christ** we are permitted that which we nevertheless see permitted by public consensus? 

Indeed, I find no other excuse than that we apply that saying of the **Persians** regarding the conduct of their King <note>An allusion to a story in Herodotus (III.31) where judges told King Cambyses that while there was no law allowing him to marry his sister, there was a law stating the King could do whatever he wished. The author is mocking the way people invent "customary" laws to bypass the Gospel.</note>: namely, that no such law is found in the **Gospel** <term>original: "Evangelium"</term>; but that there is another law among us, by which that which is seen <insert>fit</insert> is permitted. 

But my pen carries me away, I know not where—heated, I suppose, either by the proximity of so many embers, or by a just indignation against the arsonists of the **Commonwealth** <term>original: "Republica"; here referring to the community or state</term>. Let us rather invoke GOD, our GOD, a **consuming fire**, breathing out smoke from his nostrils, ejecting burning fire from his jaws, and glowing with coals <note>This vivid imagery is drawn from Psalm 18:8, describing the terrifying majesty of God's wrath.</note>. Let us rather invoke **Christ**, our Christ, walking among the fires of the lampstands, with eyes of flame, and feet as if glowing in a furnace <note>A reference to the vision of Christ in Revelation 1:14-15.</note>. Let us rather invoke the **Holy Spirit**...

<meta>catchword: Spiri-</meta>

<summary>The author critiques the hypocrisy of a society that punishes insults to men more harshly than blasphemy against God, accusing the people of following their own convenient customs rather than the Gospel. He concludes with terrifying biblical imagery of God and Christ as purifying or consuming fire.</summary>

<keywords>blasphemy, hypocrisy, Persian law, fire imagery, divine judgment, Gospel vs custom, Vaihingen fire</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page concludes the reflection on the 1617 fire of Vaihingen. On the previous page, the author lamented how human laws are often used to bypass the morality of the Gospel, even while claiming to be Christian. Here, he turns toward a prayer for spiritual purification, using the physical fire of the city as a metaphor for the refining fire of the Holy Spirit.</meta>

<page-num>275</page-num>

# VAIHINGEN, PART II
<note>original header: "VAIHINGENSE II."</note>

Let us adore with our whole soul our Spirit <note>original: "Spiritum Nostrum"; a reference to the Holy Spirit</note>, spreading itself among the faithful like tongues of fire, so that those tiny sparks of the **Divinity** <term>original: "Divinitatis"; the divine nature or essence of God</term> which He has shared with us through His ineffable mercy, He might most kindly deign to further breathe upon, cherish, and increase into the warmth of the entire Christian framework. 

However, let us clearly see Him so burn away earthly things around us—and whatever cannot withstand the furnace of testing—and so resolve them into lime and dust, that nothing at all is lost from our souls (certainly the nobler part), but rather their simplicity and perfection are increased, while the body is liberated from useless dross <note>The author employs a metallurgical metaphor: just as a refiner's fire purifies gold by removing the "scoriae" (slag or waste), the tragic fire of the city is viewed as a means to strip away worldly attachments.</note>.

But for those who believe the flesh must be fully sustained, let us pray for and supply food and clothing, roof and bedding in such a way that we may hope they will live out what remains of life—having been warned once and again of human frailty <note>original: "lubricitatis"; literally "slipperiness," referring to the unstable and fleeting nature of human existence</note>—if not more perfectly according to the **rule of Christ**, then certainly more rationally according to the **laws of nature**. May good men favor us: and in

-> **GOD** may we all be <-
-> strong! <-

<sig>H 4</sig>

<vocab>Our Spirit, of the Divinity, Christian, of Christ, laws of nature, more rationally</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes his meditation by praying that the Holy Spirit might use the tragedy of the fire to purify the souls of the faithful, stripping away earthly "dross." He ends with a plea for survivors to live more rationally and in accordance with the laws of nature, even if they cannot reach the perfection of Christ's rule.</summary>

<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>VAIHINGEN FIRE, PART II.</header>

<image-desc>An ornamental typographic headpiece consisting of symmetrical scrolling foliage, floral patterns, and heart-shaped decorative elements.</image-desc>

## On the Double Fire of Vaihingen.

Now our **VAIHINGEN** <term>A town in the Duchy of Württemberg, now known as Vaihingen an der Enz.</term> has owed *double fires and lamentable*
->*loss*<-
to an Avenging **God.**
Yet the fiery fury did not consume the entire *city:*
*The rod was merciful in the midst of the blow.* <note>This refers to the biblical concept of "divine discipline," where God punishes out of love rather than pure destruction, like a father using a switch or rod.</note>
Certainly, heavenly heat suppresses the earthly heat:
And the fire of **JOVE** <note>original: "JOVÆ"; Christian Humanist poets frequently used the name of the Roman king of the gods, Jove (Jupiter), as a poetic synonym for the Judeo-Christian God.</note> *is present in the midst of the fires.*
*Now you say, Visitor, as* **VAIHINGEN** *rises again,*
*Is the grace of God greater, or the wrath of God?*

->Johann Martin Rauscher, Professor of<-
->Humanities <note>original: "Humaniorum literarum"; refers to the study of classical literature, rhetoric, and ethics.</note> at the University of<-
->Tübingen.<-

->**T H E  E N D.**<-

<summary>A closing poem by Professor Johann Martin Rauscher interpreting the dual fires of Vaihingen as an act of divine discipline tempered by mercy. The text concludes with a rhetorical question inviting the reader to see God's grace in the city's eventual rebuilding.</summary>
<keywords>Vaihingen an der Enz, fire, Johann Martin Rauscher, University of Tübingen, divine wrath, mercy, reconstruction, Latin poetry</keywords>
